A local-force company that has learned that a rearmed Popular Forces post is expensive goes looking for the post that is not: the police post settled without its platoon, the CIDG camp with its strikers' carbines and M1918A2s, the census team's escort of constables, the hamlet whose only defence is a dozen self-defence men with shotguns. The Front's own directive of March 1964 already put the constables above the Popular Forces as a target¹⁶⁵, and this provision widens that preference into a doctrine. In the record's terms the cost is moderate: the police programme loses more constables in its unsupported posts, and the CIDG camps in the Highlands become the softest standing targets in the country. The remedy is the settled-post rule enforced without exception and the CIDG rearmed by a later decision; until then, the displacement is real.

**F004-2** The enemy moves from the rifle fight to the stand-off weapon, the satchel and the mine. A rifle assault on a rearmed post costs him more men than it did, so he substitutes 60mm and 82mm mortars fired from 1,500 metres, B40 rockets at the bunker slits, sappers with satchel charges in place of the infantry wave, and mines and booby traps on the paths the platoon patrols by day¹⁷⁸. None of these is answered by a better rifle. What the platoon's intelligence loses is the warning: an assault company forming in a treeline is often seen or heard, a mortar team firing four rounds from a rice dike and leaving is not, and a mine is invisible until it kills. In the main-force war the same substitution appears as the enemy fighting from bunkers, which a rifle does not break, and initiating contact only at ranges and in places where his RPD and RPG fire can win the first minute before our squad gun answers. The displacement is moderate: it raises the enemy's ammunition requirement and lowers the casualties he takes, and it hands our men a war of wounds from fragments in place of a war of rifle fire.

### Bottleneck migration

The provision does exactly what bottleneck analysis predicts: it removes firepower as the binding constraint on the outpost and the squad, and exposes the constraints that firepower had been masking.

**F004-3** At the outpost, the binding constraint moves from firepower at the wall to three things firepower never addressed: whether the platoon is present after dark, whether the ammunition is there, and whether a reaction force arrives within the hour. Before the provision, an outpost attacked by a local-force company with automatic weapons was often lost in the first hour because its Garands and carbines could not stop the assault at the wall. After the provision, the post that is attacked holds the first hour and runs short in the second or third, and whether it survives the third hour is decided by the district's reaction and the ammunition in the bunker. Take the post at Long Dinh on Route 4 in Dinh Tuong, overrun on 3 October 1964 three weeks after it was settled without its platoon¹⁶⁵. With the provision, the post that fell there still falls, because the platoon was not there to carry the rifles. The binding constraint in that hamlet was always presence, and the provision exposes it rather than relieving it. This is the most useful sentence in this memorandum for the provinces: the bottleneck is now who sleeps in the post and who comes when it calls, and the rifles have made that unmistakable. Major.

**F004-4** In the main-force war the binding constraint moves from fire superiority in the first minutes of a contact back to where it has always really been: finding the enemy and the enemy's control of whether battle is joined. The historical ARVN battalion often lost the first minutes of an ambush and with them the fight. The rearmed battalion holds the first minutes and then faces the enemy's real advantage, which is that he chose the place and will leave when he likes. The constraint after the provision is the hammer: helicopters, artillery within range, air on call, and a reserve that can reach the enemy before he breaks contact at dusk. In the Delta that constraint is the helicopter hours; in the Highlands it is the canopy. Moderate.

### Own-force opportunity cost

The provision is cheap in American spaces and expensive in Vietnamese training time in the worst year to spend it.

**F004-5** The American cost is about 470 spaces for training, repair and the territorial training centres, which against a theater with no ceiling is negligible. The port and road cost is modest: about 5,000 tonnes of packed 5.56mm in the first year, a fraction of a single month's general cargo through Saigon by 1966¹⁷⁹. The real cost is Vietnamese: about 600,000 men to be trained on a new rifle for three to five days in 1964¹⁶⁴, about two to three million man-days, or about 1% of a year's RVNAF man-days. It falls in the year of Khanh's coup, the Vung Tau Charter crisis and the September coup attempt, when units were being moved for political reasons and the training centres were also absorbing the Police Field Force⁸. Every Regional Forces company pulled into a training centre for a week is a district without its reaction force for that week. That is why the zeroing round brings the training to the post, and it is why the territorial rearmament takes until well into 1965 to reach the posts that most need it. What is not done in 1964 is a few weeks of other training in every ARVN battalion. Moderate, and front-loaded: by 1966 the cost is replacements and repair, not a rearmament.

### Counterfactual erasure

Four things that happened in the historical war do not happen in this one, and the attention they consumed is freed.

**F004-6** The ARVN's four years of being outgunned, the M16 crisis of 1967 and the territorial rearmament of 1968–69 are erased. From 1964 to mid-1968 the historical ARVN infantry fought AK-47s with Garands and carbines, and knew it¹⁶⁴; that gap is gone. The M16 reliability crisis that killed Marines beside their jammed rifles on Hills 861 and 881 in the spring of 1967, and the congressional inquiry it produced, does not occur: the Marines carry a mature design with a chrome-lined barrel and the right powder from the first day. The rifle line of the Improvement and Modernization programme of 1969, in which the territorial forces were finally rearmed with the M16, is erased, and the attention of the programme's designers moves to its other lines: artillery, helicopters, the training base. What the territorials' commanders did with the five years they spent asking for a modern rifle, they now spend on something else, and so does the enemy's propaganda, which loses the outgunned government soldier as a theme. Moderate: real and visible, but it erases a deficit that was one of several and not the war's deepest.

### Perverse effect

Three perverse effects, of which the first is the largest and the last the smallest.

**F004-7** Every outpost lost, every column destroyed and every deserter who takes his carbine with him arms the Front with an M4A1, a red-dot sight that runs five years on its battery and the 5.56mm it fires¹⁶⁴. The local force and the guerrillas historically armed themselves largely from captured government weapons¹⁶⁴. In the historical battle at Ba Gia in May 1965 the ARVN lost 384 individual and 10 crew-served weapons in four days; in this war those 384 are carbines with sights. If the RVNAF and territorials lose weapons at a few percent of their holdings a year in the bad years of 1964–65, that is on the order of 10,000–30,000 carbines in enemy hands by the end of 1965¹⁸⁰. There is a twist. A captured M4A1 fires only 5.56mm, which only we supply, so the local force that rearms from our posts must keep feeding its new carbines from our ammunition: captured, bought from a corrupt depot clerk or carried away from a fallen post. The Front's local force becomes a customer of our supply line, which gives the muster service and the Commission a trail to follow and gives the enemy a reason to keep the old Chinese and Soviet calibres for its main force. Moderate, because the numbers are a small fraction of what the force holds, and because the same losses historically armed the enemy with carbines and Garands that were themselves good enough for a guerrilla.

**F004-8** A Regional Forces company made effective is pulled out of its district. The rearmed Regional Forces company, with modern carbines and a better fire discipline, looks to a corps commander short of infantry like a light infantry company, and the corps commanders historically used Regional Forces companies in their operations and on their convoy escorts whenever they could. The province chief who had one good reaction company for his district loses it to a two-week sweep, and the outposts that depended on it are alone on the nights it is away. The record already notes the same perverse effect for a successful district chief promoted away from his district; this is the military version, and it is moderate, because the merit-selected chiefs have the standing to resist it and the corps commanders the power to override them.

**F004-9** The M855A1 round goes through walls. Built to penetrate 3/8-inch steel at about 400 metres¹⁶⁴, it passes through thatch, bamboo and the mud-and-wattle walls of a Delta house as if they were not there. A night fight in a hamlet, a Popular Forces platoon firing at muzzle flashes among the houses, puts rounds through the walls of the houses behind the target, and the families inside die. The.30 carbine's round, for all its faults, often stopped in the first wall. The effect is small in numbers and large in each hamlet where it happens, and it falls on exactly the population this command most needs. Minor, and to be answered by fire-control training in the zeroing round and by the rule that the platoon fights from the wall, not among the houses.

### Anticipation

**F004-10** The enemy declines the fights the rifles would win, and the improvement becomes invisible. This is the most important second-order effect of the provision and it runs exactly as it ran for the police in memorandum 002. A Viet Cong district committee that sees its local-force company lose half a platoon at the wall of a rearmed post does not attack that post again; it attacks the police post down the canal, or the post whose platoon sleeps in the district town, or it waits for the monsoon. The rearmed post is not attacked, and an outpost not attacked generates no report. The province's statistics show fewer outposts overrun, which is real, and fewer contacts, which looks like inactivity. In the main-force war the same anticipation shows as fewer ambushes of rearmed ARVN battalions in the places where they move, and more ambushes of the convoys and posts that are softer. The provision's best results are therefore the ones this headquarters cannot count. Worse, the metrics it can count will mislead in both directions: a rearmed battalion that fights its few contacts better will report more enemy dead, and the temptation to treat that body count as evidence of progress must be refused at its source. An ARVN battalion that kills more of the enemy in the fights the enemy chose has improved its fighting; it has not changed who chooses. Major, because it is the mechanism by which the provision's real effect is hidden and its false effect counted.

### Enemy detection and learning

**F004-11** The enemy detects the provision at once, reads its military meaning correctly within a season, and reads its political meaning wrongly for a year. Detection is the first contact and the first captured carbine; the rifles are in thousands of hands by March 1964, and a captured M4A1 with its sight reaches a COSVN military staff within weeks. The military reading is right and fast: COSVN's conclusion is that the government's soldiers are no longer outgunned at close range, that the rifle assault on a post now costs more, and that the answer is to attack the posts that are not rearmed, to bring the mortar and the B40 forward, to put sappers in place of assault waves and to fight from bunkers. That doctrine propagates from COSVN to province committees in a few months and to a district cell by the end of the season, and it applies everywhere the government has a post.

The political reading is wrong at first. Hanoi reads a rearmament of the RVNAF on this scale as the precursor of a larger American commitment, which it is not by itself, and as evidence that the Americans intend the ARVN to fight a long war, which is closer. For some months in 1964 the Front overrates the rearmed ARVN and is more cautious against its battalions than their leadership deserves. The error corrects itself as soon as the district committees see that a better rifle has not made a platoon go out at night or a battalion leave the road. What the enemy gets right that hurts us is the dependence: every round the rearmed force fires comes from the United States, and every post is only as good as its last resupply. Cut the road, besiege the post, and wait. Moderate.

### Enemy adaptation cost

**F004-12** The adaptations are cheap for the Front and moderately expensive for Hanoi, and on balance the exchange slightly favours us. Choosing softer targets costs the Front nothing. Mines and booby traps are made locally from duds and scrap at almost no cost. Sapper training costs time and the best young men, perhaps six months for a sapper and a year for a sapper leader¹⁸¹, and those men are drawn from the units that would otherwise have assaulted the posts. The expensive part is the stand-off weapon: mortar rounds and rockets are heavy, come down the Trail or through Sihanoukville, and raise the tonnage the external supply must carry. A local force that fights posts with 60mm and 82mm mortars and B40s instead of rifles needs perhaps two to four times as many tonnes of ammunition per attack¹⁸². Against the historical requirement of about 20 tons a day for the whole South the increment is small, but it is not nothing, and it lands on the most constrained part of the enemy's system. The rearmament cost us about 600,000 carbines and five thousand tonnes of ammunition in the first year; it costs the enemy a shift in tactics, some sappers and some tonnes on the Trail. On that exchange the provision is working, modestly, not merely busy.

### Cross-boundary feedback

**F004-13** The loop out of theater is small and positive. In the historical war roughly half of American deaths in action came from small arms¹⁸³. A mature carbine with a red-dot sight, a belt-fed squad gun and a marksman in every squad do not touch the deaths from mines, booby traps, mortars and rockets, which are the other half, and they cannot save men caught in an ambush the enemy chose well. I judge that they reduce American small-arms deaths by perhaps 5–10%, which is 2–5% of hostile deaths and on the order of 1,000–2,500 American lives over the war if the war ran its historical length, judging the effect of faster first hits and no jamming crisis on the small-arms share of about 47,000 hostile deaths. The same reasoning applied to the RVNAF, whose infantry and territorials gain much more over their historical weapons, suggests something on the order of 10,000–25,000 Vietnamese soldiers' lives over the war, from a 5–10% fall in RVNAF combat deaths in small-arms fights¹⁸⁴. On the home front the M16 scandal does not happen, no congressman holds hearings on a rifle that jams, and no letters come home about men found dead beside it. Political tolerance gains a little and the clock slows by a little; the loop returns to the theater as no more than a slightly larger margin of tolerance in 1967–68. There is no Soviet or Chinese return path of weight: the enemy was already re-equipping with the AK-47 family, and the adaptation that matters runs through rockets and mortars, which Moscow and Beijing were supplying anyway. Minor.

### Open channel

**F004-14** The cartridge case becomes evidence. Every 5.56mm round carries its lot number on its case head, and every carbine and sight a serial number on the muster roll. A Viet Cong local force fighting with captured or bought carbines leaves our ammunition lots on the ground after every attack, and a Special Branch officer who picks up the brass from a firefight and sends the lot numbers to the Commission can, in time, tell which depot or which district's issue fed them. This gives the anti-corruption programme its first physical trail from a firefight back to a seller, and gives the police a way to tell whether a local-force unit is living on captures or on purchases. It is moderate in effect and slow, and it depends on a Commission and a Special Branch that exist only because of memoranda 001 and 002; without them the lot numbers would be noted by nobody.

## §2 Part C — Counter-directional finding and strongest positive effect

**F004-15 · Counter-directional** The better the RVNAF fights with these weapons for ten years, the harder it falls if the ammunition stops. The provision teaches the ARVN and the territorials, from 1964 rather than 1968–69, to fight a small-unit war in which a belt-fed gun holds the enemy in contact and every rifleman has thirty rounds in his magazine and two hundred on his belt. That is the right way to fight with an assured supply. It is a style of war built on American ammunition¹⁶⁴, and the historical RVNAF ended its life in 1974–75 rationing rounds after American aid was cut. A force that has spent ten years learning to fight with abundant 5.56mm is not readily retrained in its last year to fight with scarce 5.56mm; its squads have no habit of economy, its posts are built around firepower, and its men's confidence rests on weapons they can no longer feed. The provision does not create the dependence, which the historical force had in full, but it deepens the habit and lengthens it by four or five years. I weigh this at moderate. It is real, it bears on the gap between assisted viability and self-sustaining defence that is the whole war, and it does not outweigh the provision's primary effect, which is to save lives and hamlets every year until that cut comes. I also ruled out, as counter-directional findings, the captured carbines (already the counter-direction of Part 1 and recorded as a perverse effect here) and the risk of an outgunned police, which is displacement rather than a reversal of the provision's own direction.

**F004-16 · Strongest positive** The territorial forces are not outgunned for the five years in which the historical countryside was lost. The Regional and Popular Forces carried the war's heaviest burden in the hamlets and took more than half of the RVNAF's combat deaths in its later years, and from 1964 to the end of 1969 they did it with carbines and Garands against an enemy carrying AK-47s¹⁶⁴. Under this provision they carry the better weapon from the first day, alongside a police programme that settles posts only with their platoon, a district chief chosen for ability who commands them and controls their resupply, and in the titled hamlets a platoon of men guarding their own land. Take Phuoc Lai on the night of 19 January 1966, where in the chronicle as it stands a Popular Forces platoon and ten constables held a new post for three hours against two companies of the 506th Local Force Battalion until an RF company arrived by canal at 01:40. That platoon in this war carries modern carbines and the constables beside it do not. The record's ratio of four posts held in five attacked under the settled-post rule¹⁶⁵ is the measure to watch, and I expect parity in firepower at the wall to raise it toward nine in ten in the posts that keep the rule¹⁸⁴, and the posts that do fall to fall later in the night, closer to the reaction force. In the three provinces around Saigon and the upper Delta where the district government of memoranda 001 and 002 is being built, that is the difference between a belt that holds through 1965 and a belt that bleeds. It is not transformative by itself, because it does nothing for the platoon that is not there. With the district government beside it, it is major: the first provision in this force that makes the night itself harder for the enemy to take.

**Employment concepts**

EC004-1 the outpost wall at night · EC004-2 the zeroing round (with P001b, P002b) · EC004-3 the squad base of fire · EC004-4 the column's first minute · EC004-5 the marksman's overwatch · EC004-6 the perimeter under the flares. Abandoned: EC004-7 marksmen pooled as battalion sniper teams (no targets under canopy; squads lost overwatch; teams killed) · EC004-8 issue through the district depot (sights sold, rifles unzeroed) · EC004-9 volume of fire as doctrine (ammunition gone in a minute) · EC004-10 the squad gun to the outposts (no linked ammunition or barrels at the posts)

## §3 Changes to existing forces

### The advisory effort and MACV staff

The advisers gain a task and a key. The zeroing round puts an American at every territorial post every five to six weeks, which is a larger and more regular presence in the territorials than the historical advisory effort achieved before the Mobile Advisory Teams of 1967–68. The MACV staff gains a small-arms and optics directorate inside J-4, and the training directorate a marksman and machine-gun instructor programme for the ARVN schools. The provision adds about 470 American spaces in all¹⁶⁴.

### US conventional forces

American units arrive from 1965 with the M4A1, the M249 and the M110A2 in place of the M14, then the M16A1, the fire team's automatic rifleman and the squad's lack of a marksman. The change is an improvement and not a transformation (Part 1 verdict). The Marine squad's three automatic weapons are belt-fed; the Army squad's two. The M60 stays in the platoon and company, the M79 and later the M203 grenade launchers in the fire teams as historically. The jamming crisis on the hills at Khe Sanh in 1967 does not occur, and American infantry win more of the first minutes of contact at 10–30 metres.

### US Special Forces and CIDG

The Special Forces teams themselves carry the M4A1, as US forces. Their CIDG strikers do not: the provision does not cover them, and they keep their carbines, Garands and M1918A2s until the historical rearmament¹⁶⁴. The camps therefore become relatively softer targets as the posts around them harden. Nam Dong in July 1964 is the pattern: a CIDG camp in the hills, attacked by a battalion at night. I will ask CINCPAC to extend the carbine to the strikers as a separate decision; it is not part of this provision.

### ARVN divisions and the JGS

The ARVN infantry is rearmed four years earlier than history and fights its battles of 1964–65 with modern carbines, a belt-fed gun in every squad and a marksman in every squad. It survives more ambushes in their first minutes and gives the enemy main force a harder time at close quarters than the historical ARVN did. It is not better led, does not go out at night more often, and does not find the enemy any more easily. The Joint General Staff gains a large logistics and training task in 1964, in the year its generals are busiest with coups.

### RF/PF and territorial security

The largest change. The Regional and Popular Forces carry modern carbines with red-dot sights from 1964, five years before their historical rearmament, and a post with its platoon present holds longer at night. They do not receive the squad gun or the marksman's rifle; the outpost keeps its BAR¹⁶⁴. Their training is brought to them by the zeroing round, and their accountability for weapons by the muster service. Their weakness stays their weakness: presence after dark, pay, leadership and the reaction force.

### Rolling Thunder

Unchanged. The provision acts on small-unit fighting in the South and has no bearing on the air campaign against the North.

### Trail interdiction

Changed at the margin. The enemy's shift to mortars and rockets against rearmed posts raises his external ammunition requirement, which makes the Trail's tonnage slightly more valuable to interdict and slightly harder for him to keep below capacity. The effect is too small to change the interdiction plan.

### Arc Light

Unchanged. B-52 strikes are planned against base areas and troop concentrations the provision does not affect.

### Airmobile formations

The airmobile cavalry and the airborne brigades carry the same weapons as other US forces. A rifle company landed in a hot landing zone has more fire in its first minutes, which matters at a landing zone where the enemy chooses when to fight, as at the Ia Drang in 1965. The airmobile concept is otherwise unchanged.

### Riverine and coastal forces

The Vietnamese River Assault Groups and the junk force are covered as RVNAF and carry the M4A1. The American riverine and coastal forces carry it as US forces. Boarding parties searching junks and sampans have a lighter, faster weapon; nothing else changes.

### Phoenix / Phung Hoang

Indirectly strengthened. The Special Branch and the police are not rearmed, but the Popular Forces platoons that protect them and their informants are (Part A, the identify-to-neutralise chain). The lot-number trail gives the Special Branch one new kind of physical evidence.

### Third-country forces

The provision does not cover the Korean, Australian, New Zealand, Thai or Philippine contingents. The Korean divisions, which historically received M16s ahead of the ARVN, will arrive in 1965 with M1 rifles and carbines and find the ARVN better armed than they are. Seoul will ask for the M4A1 within weeks. I will support the request; the political cost is negligible and it reverses a resentment the ARVN carried in the historical war.

### Prior provisions

- **P001a** · Land-to-the-Tiller Programme: the escort Regional Forces companies that go out with the titling teams are rearmed, and the Popular Forces platoons recruited from titled families guard their land with a rifle that is not outgunned (Part A, the titled family and the armed son).
- **P001b** · Anti-Corruption Programme: the pay and muster verification service adds weapons to its count, serial number to soldier, and the Commission gains a trail from captured ammunition lots to depots; the depot issue of early 1964 is its first test.
- **P002a** · National Police Reform: the constables and the Police Field Force keep their carbines, so the post's defence rests more than ever on the Popular Forces platoon beside it, and the unrearmed police become the Front's preferred target.
- **P002b** · Provincial Administration Reform: the merit-selected district chief controls the territorials' ammunition and escorts the zeroing teams; weapon accountability becomes one measure of a chief, and he resists the loss of his rearmed Regional Forces companies to corps operations.

Unchanged: P003a, P003b.

# Memorandum 004 — Part 3 of 3 · §4 The course of the war · §5 Delta ledger · §6 Changed / not changed

*Audit — Memo 004, Part 3/3 · v1.0.4 · phases revised: 4 (P1, P2, P3, P6), nulled: 2 · events created or changed: 16 (P1 5, P2 5, P3 3, P6 3; floor 3/phase) · new operations: 1 · chains reaching link 3: 5 (floor 3) · phases carrying a §2 finding: 4/4 · strongest positive grounded: yes · clock stated: 2 phase(s) · enemy initiative: 1 · free play: 0 · ripple flags: 1 (P3→P4) · end state: unchanged (rung a) · structural deviation: none*

## §4.0 The course of the war

The war I took over on the first day of 1964 was being lost in the hamlets, and the men losing it were carrying the wrong rifles. That second fact was never the first cause of anything, and I do not pretend now that it was. But it was true, and the soldiers knew it before the generals did. A Popular Forces platoon behind a mud wall in Long An with eight-round Garands and a couple of tired BARs was facing a local-force company with automatic weapons, and every man in the platoon understood the arithmetic of the first thirty seconds. In this war that arithmetic changed on the first day, in the same week as the land decree, the police decree and the first flights of the aircraft that see the enemy's rear. Those four things together are the war I now command, and this memorandum is about the last of them and the least of them.

The first months of 1964 were spent on ranges. The ships came into the Khanh Hoi wharves from January with carbines, sights, squad guns and marksman rifles, and the issue went out under one rule: no new weapon leaves the depot until the old one comes in, and no carbine goes to a post until it has been zeroed with the man who will carry it. The Delta drew first, because the Delta was where the war was being lost, and the RF and PF of Long An and Dinh Tuong were on the ranges in January, beside the titling teams' first surveyors and the first constables of the rural police. Khanh's coup at the end of the month cost us nine days and a fifth of the training, as every coup cost something; companies went back to garrison the province towns with carbines they had not zeroed. By the summer about 310,000 carbines were in Vietnamese hands. The old Garands and carbines came back through the gate, and about a third of them went out again, over my objection, to the police, the Combat Youth and the hamlet militia, who were not covered and wanted something. I lost that argument and was right to lose it gracefully. I was also right on the substance, and the Front proved it within a year.

The first thing the rifles did was let the outposts hold. In the held belt, where the police posts were settling beside Popular Forces platoons and the titles were being issued under their protection, a post with its platoon now held about nine times in ten when it was attacked, where the programme's design had promised four in five. The night at Long Huu in November 1964, when a company of the 516th Local Force Battalion came over the bund and broke on thirty-round magazines that did not pause to reload, was told and retold in every district office in Kien Hoa. It is the strongest positive effect of everything in this memorandum, and it is invisible in every statistic this headquarters kept, because a post that is not overrun produces no report. I record it here at its full weight. The men who hold the hamlets at night were not outgunned for the five years in which, historically, the countryside was lost to men who outgunned them.

The second thing the rifles did was teach the Front where else to go. It learned in a season, faster than I had planned for. From December 1964 its district companies stopped assaulting posts that had their platoons and went for everything that did not: posts settled ahead of security, constables on the road between villages, the CIDG camps, and the militia carrying the Garands we had handed in. Against the posts that held it brought the 60mm mortar from three hundred metres and the mine on the path to the morning market. Two-thirds of the constables killed in 1965 died outside their posts. And every post that did fall, every deserter who walked away with his weapon, and every battalion ambushed in the summer gave the Front our carbines, between ten and thirty thousand of them by the end of 1965. They were the best rifles any guerrilla in the country carried, and they fired our ammunition.

The summer of 1965 came as it came historically, because the rifles did not change the one thing that decided it: the enemy chose where to fight. At Binh Gia at the turn of the year the 271st Regiment took the village and waited on the road for the general reserve, and the general reserve drove down it. The 4th Marine Battalion was ambushed in the rubber at thirty metres as it was historically, and its commander died in the first minutes as he did historically. The difference was the next quarter of an hour. Three squad guns to a platoon put a base of fire into the trees, and the companies closed into perimeters instead of being destroyed in the order they had marched. About 65 Marines died instead of 122, and the battalion was in the line again in February. At Ba Gia in May the 1st Battalion, 51st Infantry was cut in two instead of wiped out, and at Dong Xoai in June about 270 of the 7th Airborne Battalion's 470 men fought their way back from Thuan Loi, where historically about 150 came. These were still defeats. The Viet Cong held the field at each, and the lesson Hanoi drew, that it could choose the field and the ARVN would keep driving to it, was the right lesson whatever our soldiers carried. Westmoreland's June request rested on the PAVN regiments coming into the Highlands as much as on the battalions lost, and the July decision was taken on the same day, for the same reasons, as it was historically. I did not expect the rifles to change it and they did not.

The Americans came with the same weapons, and for them the change was smaller and fairly exact. At X-Ray the perimeter held as it held historically, and at Albany the 2nd Battalion, 7th Cavalry was ambushed in the elephant grass and lost about 125 men instead of 155. The head of the column formed a perimeter in minutes behind its squad guns; the companies strung out behind it could not see each other at twenty metres, and a machine gun is no use to a squad that cannot find its targets or its friends. That sentence is the honest limit of this whole provision in the main-force war. The B3 Front's study of the Ia Drang noted that the American squads now carried "a light machine gun in every cell", and concluded that the belt-grabbing assault must be made closer still, at night, or not at all. It was made closer.

The Marines on 881 South in May 1967 did not die beside rifles they were trying to clear. In this war there was no M16 crisis, no subcommittee, no letters home about a rifle that jammed, and about thirty fewer dead in the Hill Fights. Over the whole war I believe the rifles saved between about 1,000 and 2,500 American lives, and between 10,000 and 25,000 South Vietnamese, though I sign those estimates knowing they are estimates. They are a few percent of what this war cost. They are also real men, and I do not think a commander who has signed the letters is entitled to call that small.

The rearmament made its own trouble in Phase II, in ways that show how a good thing moves a bottleneck rather than removing it. The Regional Forces of the western Delta were good enough by 1966 that the IV Corps headquarters borrowed thirty-four of their companies for a season against the U Minh. They fought well, and the forty-one outposts of Chuong Thien that had depended on them for a reaction force lost twenty-three posts in four months. The Front had not needed to plan anything; it had watched the boats carry the companies away. The province chiefs' returns came to Saigon, and when CORDS was formed in 1967 it carried a rule that this war wrote from its own losses: the Regional Force company belongs to its province, and the corps borrows it for thirty days with the province chief's consent or not at all. In the same year the Commission convicted the commander of the 9th Division's ammunition point at Sa Dec and two Cholon dealers, on evidence the war itself supplied: the lot numbers on cartridge cases picked up behind a Viet Cong firing position in the Plain of Reeds, matched against the issue ledgers the muster service had kept since 1964. A cartridge case on a battlefield could now put an officer in prison. The leak of ammunition to the Front did not stop; it moved from the depots to the units in the field.

Hanoi decided on the General Offensive in the summer and autumn of 1967 for Hanoi's reasons, and in this war one of those reasons destroyed a piece of evidence it needed. A study group of the General Staff's combat research department had concluded in the spring, from three years of after-action reports, that the southern army's infantry and territorials no longer broke in close combat and that the local forces could not take district towns from them without main-force support. Its senior officers were arrested in December in the second wave of the Revisionist Anti-Party purge, for whose staff they served on rather than for what they had written, and the study went into the files with them. The offensive was planned, as it was historically, on the assumption that the ARVN would dissolve on contact.

It did not dissolve historically, and here it had been better armed than its attackers for four years. Tet came on the same night, against the same towns, with the same forces; the cities were the cities, and Hue was a month of street fighting in the rain whatever our squads carried. The Marines' marksmen killed machine gunners in the windows of the treasury from two hundred metres, and the 106s, the Ontos and men going through walls took the blocks, as they did historically. The difference was in the district towns, which the first wave attacked with local forces against an RF company or two and a PF platoon behind a compound wall. About a third of those attacks never got inside the wall. At Cai Lay the 311th RF Company held the district compound all night against a battalion that had planned to be through the town by four in the morning. With the land programme's Popular Forces reporting the columns at Can Tho and the Special Branch's files in the Delta towns, it made the first wave in IV Corps a shorter and less destructive thing than it was historically. In Washington it changed nothing. The President's decision of 31 March was taken on the same day for the same reasons, because the American clock was spent by the progress campaign's collapse and by the casualty lists, and the rifles had moved neither by enough to show.

After Tet the gap the rifles had opened over history began to close from the other side. The historical ARVN received the M16 in 1968 and its territorials in 1969, and from then on the difference between this army and that one was the squad gun, the marksman and four years' practice with both. The mobilization of June 1968 armed its new territorials from stock within a month, and found that the constraint on the expansion was sergeants, not rifles. Through Vietnamization, the incursion, Lam Son 719 and the Easter Offensive, the rifles are present and do not decide anything. Quang Tri still fell in May 1972 because its defence failed at the top; An Loc and Kontum still held because the B-52s held them. A belt-fed gun at An Loc made each assault on the perimeter cost the PAVN more, and the PAVN's answer was its artillery, which no rifle touched.

The rifles came back into the story at the end, and in the wrong direction. The army that faced the ration of 1974 had been built for ten years around the belt-fed gun and the abundance of American ammunition, four years longer than the historical one. When the Joint General Staff cut the squad to its basic load for a month, the squad gun went silent first: two hundred rounds a gun for a month, two minutes of fire. Battalions that had learned to break contacts with volume avoided contacts they could not break, and the marksmen's rifles went into the arms rooms for want of match ammunition. The divisions of I and II Corps hoarded linked 5.56mm against orders, about nine million rounds by the end of the year, and in March 1975 the hoards were lost intact at Hue, Da Nang and Qui Nhon, while the 18th Division at Xuan Loc fought on what Long Binh could still send. It held for twelve days, as it did historically. Its marksmen made the 341st Division's battalions go to ground at four hundred metres in the rubber east of the town. The PAVN bypassed it, as it did historically.

Saigon fell on 30 April 1975. Rung (a), collapse, on the historical day. I have now written four memoranda in which the Republic became a better state, a better-policed state, a better-informed command and now a better-armed army, and in which it fell on the same day. I do not find that discouraging so much as clarifying. Each of these provisions fixed something real. None of them touched the three things that decided the end: Hanoi's will and its sanctuaries, the American clock, and the Republic's dependence on an American supply line that Congress could close. The rifles made the war cheaper in lives on our side, in the hamlets above all, and they did it from the first week. They did not decide when the enemy fought, and they deepened the dependence that killed the Republic in the end.

## §4.P1 Phase I — The advisory war and escalation (Jan 1964 – Jul 1965)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: POP.night_control +1 · POP.gvn_legitimacy +1 · FIND.main_force +1 · FIND.vci +1 · ARVN.effectiveness +1.*

The war that COMUSMACV inherits on 1 January 1964 is an advisory war that is being lost. Two months earlier the coup of 1 November 1963 had killed Ngo Dinh Diem and his brother Nhu, and with them went the province chiefs, the Strategic Hamlet Program and most of the administrative continuity the Republic of Vietnam possessed. The Military Assistance Command, Vietnam, under General Paul D. Harkins, is a headquarters of advisers, helicopter companies and support troops; the United States has about 23,300 military personnel in the country by the end of 1964, none of them formed into combat units, and 147 die by hostile action that year¹⁸⁵. The fighting is done by the Army of the Republic of Vietnam, the Regional and Popular Forces, and the Civilian Irregular Defense Group camps of the US Special Forces, against a Viet Cong movement that has rebuilt itself since 1959 on the political infrastructure left from the war against France and on cadres regrouped to the North in 1954 and now returning down the Trail.

Phase I runs from January 1964 to the end of July 1965. Its shape is a double collapse and a double response. In Saigon, the state comes apart: between January 1964 and June 1965 the government changes hands by coup or forced resignation repeatedly, and the generals spend more of their energy on each other than on the countryside. In the field, the Viet Cong move from the guerrilla phase to the mobile-war phase, forming regiments and then a division in the provinces around Saigon, and at Binh Gia, Song Be, Ba Gia and Dong Xoai they destroy ARVN battalions in set-piece battles. In Hanoi, the Politburo decides that the moment for decisive victory has come and commits regular PAVN regiments to the South for the first time. The American response comes in two steps: from the air, beginning with the reprisal strikes of August 1964 and February 1965 and hardening into Rolling Thunder in March 1965; and on the ground, beginning with two Marine battalions sent to guard an airfield in March 1965 and ending with the President's decision of July 1965 to commit US ground forces to the war in strength.

One thing in this phase is new. On the first day of 1964 the junta promulgates a land reform that ends rent on tenanted rice land, gives the land free to the man who works it and confirms to him whatever the Viet Minh or the Front had already given, together with an independent commission against corruption backed by an American power to suspend aid. Neither touches the main force, and the double collapse in Saigon and in the field runs much as before. What changes, slowly and only where the government holds the hamlets, is what the government's arrival means to a tenant: by the end of July 1965 about 330,000 hectares are titled, the rent stop holds in most government-held districts, and the first musters have shown the RVNAF that it is about 41,000 men smaller than its rolls¹⁸⁶. The reforms also give the coup-makers a new grievance and Hanoi a new reason to hurry.

Every one of the political constraints that will shape the war is drawn in this phase: the restricted zones around Hanoi and Haiphong, the bar on ground operations in Laos and Cambodia, the reliance on presidential approval for targets in the North, and the decision to fight without calling up the reserves. So is the enemy's basic answer to American firepower: the decision to close in, choose the time of battle, and keep his base areas beyond the border.

A second change runs beside the first. The same decrees of 1 January gave the Republic a rural police and a system of choosing its province and district chiefs by examination, from a ranked list, for at least two years. Khanh's coup tested both within a month and both survived it. By July 1965 about 1,100 village police posts stand in the held belt, about two-thirds of the chiefs have been appointed through the list, and the Special Branch has begun to take the Front's village committees on evidence; the Front has killed about 410 constables, almost all in posts settled ahead of night security¹⁶⁵. None of it touches the main-force war or Saigon's coups.

A third change runs from the same first day, and it is a change in what this command can see rather than in what the Republic is. Two U-2S and four Global Hawks begin flying in January 1964, and the enemy's rear stops being a matter of guesswork. The coastal picket, TIDE TABLE, sees the trawlers of the North's maritime group every time they run south and closes the sea route within a year; Hanoi moves its southern supply to Sihanoukville about eighteen months before it did historically. On the night of 4 August 1964 the radar shows no torpedo boats near the destroyers, Pierce Arrow is not flown, and the Southeast Asia Resolution passes on the real attack of 2 August alone. The SA-2 sites are mapped as they are built, and Washington still declines to strike them. None of it touches the coups in Saigon, the summer offensive or the July decision.

A fourth change is in the hands of the men who fight. From January 1964 the ARVN, the Rangers, the Airborne, the Marines and the Regional and Popular Forces draw modern carbines with red-dot sights, a belt-fed squad gun in every regular squad and a marksman's rifle beside it. The territorials who hold the hamlets at night are no longer outgunned by the guerrillas who come over the wall, and in the held belt more posts hold through the night. The battles of the summer of 1965 are still lost, at Binh Gia, Ba Gia and Dong Xoai, because the enemy still chooses where and when to fight; they are lost at a smaller price, and no battalion of the general reserve is wiped out. The outposts that fall give the Front the same carbines.

#### The Land-to-the-Tiller Decree and the Commission Against Corruption
*1 Jan 1964 · Saigon · new · EV001-1*
On 1 January 1964 the Military Revolutionary Council of General Duong Van Minh, with Prime Minister Nguyen Ngoc Tho's cabinet, promulgated two decree-laws drafted with the US Mission over the preceding weeks. The first ended the collection of rent on tenanted rice land from the coming main-season harvest, made every present tiller the provisional owner of the land he worked up to 3 hectares in the Delta and 1 hectare in the Central Lowlands, set the landlord's retention of tenanted land at zero and his compensation at 2.5 times the land's annual paddy yield, 20% in cash and the rest in eight-year bonds indexed to the paddy price, and confirmed by name every distribution the Viet Minh or the Front had made to the man now tilling the land. The second created the Commission Against Corruption under a Commissioner appointed for six years, Judge Truong Vinh Khoa of the Saigon Court of Appeal, a Special Court for Corruption, a Pay and Muster Verification Service, and a Joint US–GVN Aid Compliance Board whose American co-chair, the Deputy Chief of Mission, could suspend aid to a named command, importer or province and whose concurrence was needed to remove the Commissioner⁷.

Minh read the land decree on Radio Saigon that evening; its first sentence was the confirmation of the Front's distributions. Within a week the Vietnamese Information Service's loudspeaker teams and leaflets had carried it to the district towns of the Delta and the coastal Centre. The landowners' associations of Saigon and Can Tho protested to the council within days, and the protest was carried by officers of landed families who sat on it. Two USAF-contracted survey aircraft began photographing the rice lands from Tan Son Nhut on 13 January, tied to ground control laid by an Army topographic detachment from the old French triangulation; they finished the Delta in early April and the coastal Centre in May¹⁸⁶.

#### The Police and Provincial Administration decrees; the first list
*1 Jan – 27 Apr 1964 · Saigon · new · EV002-1*
On 1 January 1964, in the same session that promulgated the land and anti-corruption decrees, the Military Revolutionary Council of General Duong Van Minh promulgated two more decree-laws drafted with the US Mission and the aid mission's Public Safety Division. The first rebuilt the National Police around the village: a resident post of 8 to 12 constables in every village the government held, a Police Special Branch with sole charge of intelligence on the Viet Cong infrastructure, a Police Field Force to be raised at once rather than in 1966, a national identity card and family census, a pay and career statute, and magistrate review of every arrest within 72 hours, with detention without trial capped at two years and reviewed every six months. The Directorate General was given to Vo Dinh Cuong, a career inspector of the old Sûreté who had served in the police under the French and under Diem and had kept out of Nhu's political service, for a fixed term removable only for cause¹⁶⁵. The second decree created the Provincial Administration Selection Board, seven members under Judge Dinh Quang Tuyen of the Court of Cassation, and an Inspectorate of Provincial Administration, and made unlawful the appointment of any province chief, district chief or deputy for administration who was not taken from the Board's ranked list⁸.

The two decrees were read on Radio Saigon on the evening of 1 January after the land decree, and drew less notice than it did; the landowners protested the land decree, and the officers who would lose their provinces said nothing yet, because there was nothing yet to lose. The British Advisory Mission under R. G. K. Thompson, in Saigon since 1961, lent four Special Branch officers from the Federation of Malaysia to write the Special Branch's first manuals; the first 2,500 recruits a month entered the police training centres at Vung Tau and Da Lat from February. The Board's written examination was sat on 2 March at the National Institute of Administration by about 1,640 officers and civil servants, of whom about 1,000 were judged credible after the three-day assessments held through March and April¹⁶⁵.

On 27 April 1964 the Board published its first list in the official gazette: ranked candidates for 31 posts, the 6 province chiefs and 25 district chiefs whom the Inspectorate's first sweep had found worst, nine of them in Khanh's "acting" appointments of February. Khanh's government appointed 27 of the 31 from the first name on each list, 3 from the second and 1 from the third; it refused no list. By 31 December 1964 about 95 of the Republic's roughly 290 chiefs had been appointed through the list, a third of the whole, and the Board's register of candidates had become the first record the Republic had ever kept of what its officers could actually do¹⁶⁵.

#### Operation TIDE TABLE
*6 Jan – 30 Jun 1964 · The II Corps coast from Qui Nhon to Cam Ranh Bay (Binh Dinh, Phu Yen and Khanh Hoa Provinces) and the Ca Mau coast of An Xuyen Province · new · EV003-1*
The first Global Hawk orbit went up from Clark Air Base on 6 January 1964 and took station off the II Corps coast, about 60 nautical miles out between Qui Nhon and Cam Ranh Bay, with its radar's maritime mode sweeping a belt of sea about 150 nautical miles deep⁹. The operation was named TIDE TABLE. Its quarry was the steel-hulled trawlers of about 100 tons run south from Haiphong and the Do Son peninsula by the PAVN's maritime transport group, Group 759, which became Group 125 of the PAVN Navy in the course of 1964. Its hunters were few: the Vietnamese Navy's Sea Force, with a handful of PC-461-class submarine chasers and PCE escorts out of Qui Nhon and Nha Trang, the motorised and sail junks of the Junk Force's coastal divisions, and, when the Seventh Fleet lent them, a US destroyer or two off the coast. Before January nobody had been looking for the trawlers at all; the coast is about 2,000 kilometres long and carried tens of thousands of fishing junks [M-TNA].

The first track came on the night of 18–19 February. A contact moving at a steady ten knots on a course that had begun in the Gulf of Tonkin two days earlier, running 80 nautical miles offshore past Quang Ngai, turned west at dusk toward the Phu Yen coast; it did not stop, did not fish and did not change speed. The exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut flagged it at 1940, and the Vietnamese Navy's coastal command at Nha Trang sent a PC-461 from her patrol line off Tuy Hoa. The radar held the trawler for seven hours, so the patrol craft did not have to search; she had only to steam. She came up with the trawler at 0310 about four miles off Vung Ro Bay, under a low overcast and in a moderate northeast swell. The trawler's crew ran her toward the beach and fired her demolition charges, as Group 759's standing orders required; the forward hold blew, the after hold did not. A boarding party pulled out about 30 tons of cargo before the fire reached it: Chinese Type 56 carbines and assault rifles, B-40 (RPG-2) launchers and their rockets, 82mm mortar rounds and about 400,000 rounds of 7.62mm ammunition. Seven of the crew were taken from the water, two of them wounded; nine died in the explosion or drowned. The Vietnamese lost one sailor killed and four wounded to small-arms fire from the beach, where a VC reception party had waited with porters¹⁸⁷.

By the end of June the orbit had tracked eleven contacts that the cell judged to be trawlers, on the II Corps coast and on the Ca Mau peninsula, where the landings went into the mangrove creeks of the Bo De and the Cua Lon. Four were intercepted: two scuttled or blew up, one was taken nearly intact off Binh Dinh in April by the destroyer USS *Edsall*'s boarding party with a Vietnamese liaison officer, and one ran into the Bo De ahead of a junk division and landed most of its cargo. Six turned back when a patrol came over the horizon, which the radar saw as a reversal of course at about the time the trawler's own radar would have detected the patrol. One landed unobserved in May, on a night when the orbit's aircraft had diverted to Clark with a generator fault and the relief was four hours late¹⁸⁷. The casualty figures above are counts of bodies and prisoners and are reliable; the cargo tonnages are estimates from what was recovered and from the prisoners' statements of their loads, and carry a margin of about a third¹⁸⁸.

The prisoners told the rest. Interrogated at Nha Trang, the crew of the April capture described the landing points, the signal lamps, the reception parties and about two years of earlier voyages, and the Vietnamese Navy found two of the landing beaches in Phu Yen and Khanh Hoa from their directions¹⁸⁷. TIDE TABLE did not starve the Front; the tonnage it stopped in six months was a small fraction of what moved by sea and through Laos. What it ended was the cheapest and most direct route by which heavy weapons reached the coastal provinces of II Corps and the lower Delta, and it ended it by being seen, which is the subject of the next event in this chapter.

#### ◆ Operation IRON GATE
*6 Jan – 30 Jun 1964 · Saigon port and Tan Son Nhut; the Quang Trung Training Center at Hoc Mon, Gia Dinh Province; the division training centres and the province RF/PF training camps of IV and III Corps, then countrywide · new · EV004-1*
The first ship of the rearmament, MSTS *Private Joe P. Martinez*, berthed at the Khanh Hoi wharves in Saigon on 6 January 1964 with about 41,000 M4A1 carbines, their Aimpoint CompM5 sights, 2,400 M249 light machine guns and 320 M110A2 marksman rifles, and about 380 tonnes of 5.56mm and 7.62mm ammunition. MACV called the issue Operation IRON GATE, because the first rule of it was that no new weapon left a depot until the old one came in through the gate in exchange. The second rule was the zeroing range. Every company that drew carbines drew them by name against its muster roll, fired each one at 25 metres until the dot sat on the point of aim, and handed in its M1 Garands, M1 and M2 carbines, BARs and Thompsons before it left. Lieutenant Colonel Arthur L. Venn, chief of the small-arms new-equipment training team, set the course at five days for an infantry company and three for a Popular Forces platoon, and refused to shorten it when the corps commanders asked¹⁸⁴.

The order of issue was the order of the war's danger. The ARVN 7th, 9th and 21st Divisions in the Delta and the Regional and Popular Forces of Long An, Dinh Tuong, Kien Hoa and the Bassac provinces drew first, in January and February; the Airborne Brigade, the Marine battalions and the Ranger battalions of the general reserve in February and March; I and II Corps from April. Khanh's coup of 30 January stopped the issue for nine days and cost more: three of the division commanders who had sent their companies to the ranges were replaced, and their successors recalled the companies to garrison the province towns against a counter-coup before the course was finished. Venn's teams counted about 2.4 million Vietnamese training man-days in the first half of 1964 and reckoned that about a fifth of them were lost to the coup and its aftermath, in units that drew carbines and went back to their posts without zeroing them¹⁸⁴.

By 30 June about 310,000 carbines had been issued, about 180,000 of them to the RF and PF, with about 2,900 squad guns and 1,800 marksman rifles to the regular infantry; the marksmen's course at Quang Trung, three weeks long, had graduated its first 1,100. The depots took back about 290,000 old weapons. About a third of them went, at the Directorate's insistence and over this headquarters' objection, to the National Police, the Combat Youth and the hamlet militia, who were not covered by the rearmament and wanted something better than shotguns; the rest were crated at the Go Vap arsenal. On the ranges of the Delta the change was visible in an afternoon. A Popular Forces platoon at Can Duoc that had fired its Garands at a 100-metre target and hit it about once in four shots put most of its rounds into the same target by the third morning with carbines its smallest men could hold steady, and asked to be allowed to keep the spent cases as proof to show the village. The Front's province committee in Long An reported the new weapons to the region in February and called them a "trick to buy the puppet soldiers' loyalty"; it ordered its local-force companies to capture one for study, and the first was taken on 22 March in an ambush at Rach Kien¹⁸⁴.

#### Khanh's coup
*30 Jan 1964 · Saigon · changed from history · EV000-1*
Before dawn on 30 January 1964 Major General Nguyen Khanh, commanding I Corps, took power from the Military Revolutionary Council of General Duong Van Minh without firing a shot. He acted with Generals Tran Thien Khiem and Nguyen Van Thieu and Colonels Nguyen Chanh Thi and Do Mau. Generals Tran Van Don, Le Van Kim, Ton That Dinh and Mai Huu Xuan were put under house arrest on a charge of plotting a French-backed neutralist settlement, and Prime Minister Nguyen Ngoc Tho was detained. The one death was Major Nguyen Van Nhung, Minh's aide, executed after the takeover. Khanh kept Minh as a figurehead chief of state. The generals were tried in closed session on 28–29 May and found guilty only of "lax morality"¹⁸⁹.

The coup was not caused by the decrees of 1 January; it was the product of the generals' rivalries and Khanh's ambition, and it came on the historical date. But the reforms shaped what followed. Officers of landed families and commanders who drew ghost pay gave Khanh their quiet support in the hope of a government that would let the decrees lapse. In February Khanh asked Commissioner Khoa to bring corruption charges against the Dalat generals; Khoa replied that the Commission had no evidence against them and would bring no case without it. Khanh moved to replace him, and the American co-chair of the Aid Compliance Board refused the concurrence the decree required¹⁸⁶. The generals went before a military court instead, on the "lax morality" charge of the historical record.

Washington was surprised but quick to back the new man; President Johnson sent Khanh a personal note on 2 February¹⁸⁹, which in this war carried the condition, at the urging of the US Mission and this headquarters, that the United States would support no government that repealed either decree¹⁸⁶. Khanh, who needed American backing more than he needed the landlords, accepted it and kept both. The coup mattered less for what Khanh did than for the precedent it set: a second violent change of government within three months, carried out by officers against officers, and accepted by the United States. Khanh moved, as every coup-maker had, to place his men in the provinces, and the decree of 1 January now stood in his way. In March, Secretary of Defense McNamara visited and endorsed Khanh, and NSAM 288 of 17 March 1964 committed the United States to the survival of an independent, non-communist South Vietnam. In June 1964 General William C. Westmoreland, Harkins's deputy since January, took command of MACV, and General Maxwell D. Taylor replaced Henry Cabot Lodge as ambassador in July.

In this war Khanh's coup was the first test of the police and provincial decrees, and it came thirty days after them, before the Board had published a list. On the morning of 30 January Khanh's officers detained the new Director General of National Police, Vo Dinh Cuong, as they had detained the police chief on the historical course, and in the first week of February Khanh named "acting" chiefs in eleven provinces and districts. The US Mission and this headquarters treated the two decrees as part of the condition Johnson's note attached to the land and anti-corruption decrees: the Aid Compliance Board gave notice that a province whose chief was not taken from the list would receive no counterpart funds once the first list was published, and Cuong was released on 2 February and restored to his office. Khanh kept the Board, because he had no candidate for its chairmanship and saw a reputation for reform in it, and kept his acting chiefs until the list replaced nine of the eleven in April and May; the other two placed well enough to be appointed from it¹⁶⁵. It was a partial failure of the reform's first test and a partial success, and it set the pattern that held until 1970: the Board survived each government because the government that inherited it found it cheaper to keep than to break.

#### ◆ The first posts and the Front's answer
*1 Mar 1964 – 31 Jul 1965 · The Delta along the Bassac and the Mekong, the provinces around Saigon, and the coastal Centre from Quang Nam to Binh Dinh · new · EV002-2*
The first village police post was settled at Phu Tan in An Giang on 1 March 1964, in the Hoa Hao country where the titling teams would begin six weeks later, and the posts thereafter followed the titling line into villages the government held at night. Each was a brick house with a radio, an AN/PRC-10 or one of the HT-1 village sets, ten constables armed with M1 carbines and a Thompson or a BAR, and, by the programme's rule, a Popular Forces platoon in the same village. From February the platoon beside the post carried M4A1 carbines with red-dot sights, drawn under IRON GATE; the constables, whom the rearmament did not cover, kept the carbines of 1950¹⁸⁴. About 600 posts were manned by the end of 1964 and about 1,100 by the end of July 1965. Behind them came the census teams, registering families house by house and issuing the card and the household booklet, and the night check, in which a constable and a PF corporal walked the hamlet after dark with the booklets and counted heads⁸. By July 1965 about 45 percent of the accessible adult population was registered¹⁶⁵.

The Front read the programme within weeks, as this headquarters had expected. A COSVN directive of March 1964, captured in Dinh Tuong in October, named the rural police a target above the Popular Forces, ordered the district committees to kill constables in their first months before they learned the village, to put agents into the Special Branch while it was recruiting fast, and to confiscate the new identity cards in the villages the Front reached at night. On the night of 3 October 1964 a local-force company overran the post at Long Dinh on Route 4 in Dinh Tuong, which had been settled three weeks earlier without its PF platoon, and killed seven of its nine constables; their carbines armed a guerrilla platoon that was still carrying them when it was destroyed in 1966. By the end of July 1965 about 85 posts had been overrun or abandoned, about 410 constables killed and about 300 weapons lost, almost all in posts settled ahead of night security or without the PF platoon the rule required¹⁶⁵.

Where the platoon was there, it now held. Of the posts attacked that had their platoon and a reaction force within an hour by road or water, about nine in ten held, against about four in five that the programme's design would have given them with the platoons' old weapons. The case that the Delta's advisers told for a year was the night of 17–18 November 1964 at Long Huu in Kien Hoa, when a company of the 516th Local Force Battalion came over the bund at 0140 against a post whose PF platoon had zeroed its carbines in March. The platoon's two BARs had been replaced by carbines with 30-round magazines, and the company's first assault, which on the Front's own experience should have reached the wall while the defenders reloaded their Garands, broke on fire that did not pause; the second was not pressed, and the company withdrew before the Regional Forces boat company from Ham Long arrived at 0320, leaving eleven dead on the bund and taking its wounded. The Front learned that lesson faster than this headquarters expected. From December its district companies stopped assaulting posts with platoons and went for what was left: posts without platoons, constables on the road between villages, the CIDG camps and the hamlet militia carrying the Garands the RVNAF had handed in, and, against the posts that held, the 60mm mortar from 300 metres and the mine on the path to the morning market. About two-thirds of the constables killed in 1965 died outside their posts¹⁸⁴.

The weapons the Front took changed with its targets. Of the roughly 300 weapons lost with the police posts, nearly all were the constables' old carbines; but across the countryside the outposts that did fall, the Popular Forces deserters who walked away with their arms, and the ARVN battalions ambushed in the summer of 1965 gave the Front between about 10,000 and 30,000 M4A1 carbines by the end of 1965, with their sights¹⁸⁴. They were the best rifles any guerrilla in the country carried, and each one needed the Republic's own 5.56mm to fire.

The Special Branch's first harvest came in the held belt. By July 1965 its district sections had arrested about 1,900 people as members of the infrastructure, each on three independent sources, and the magistrates had released about a third of them within the 72 hours, which the Branch resented and this headquarters did not. In February 1965 the Branch found its first penetration, a clerk in the Dinh Tuong provincial section who had been copying informant cards for the province committee's security section since the previous summer; eleven informants named on them had been killed¹⁶⁵. The posts, the census and the files were working where the government held the night and were a list of targets where it did not, which is what the programme's design had said and what the casualty returns now showed.

#### The first titling teams
*16 Mar – 31 Dec 1964 · An Giang, Phong Dinh, Vinh Long, Gia Dinh, Bien Hoa and Long An Provinces, and the coastal Centre from Quang Nam to Binh Dinh · new · EV001-2*
The first 40 mobile titling teams of about 25 men each took the field on 16 March 1964, working outward from province capitals and main roads into villages the district could reinforce by day, each escorted by a Regional Forces company armed with M1 and M2 carbines and M1918A2 automatic rifles. The first titles were handed over at Long Xuyen in An Giang on 21 April, in the Hoa Hao country where the Front was weakest, and the first in a contested district at Phung Hiep in Phong Dinh on 11 May. By July 70 teams were in the field, the most the provinces could escort without stripping their daytime reaction forces. They moved by road, in the LCM-6 and LCVP craft of the River Assault Groups and by hired sampan, and to villages beyond both by CH-21C and UH-1B helicopters when the operations staff would release them¹⁸⁶.

By 31 December 1964 titles had been issued for about 210,000 hectares to about 170,000 families, about a fifth of the target, almost all of it in hamlets held by the government at night or contested only lightly: An Giang, the riverine districts of Phong Dinh and Vinh Long along the Bassac, the inner districts of Gia Dinh and Bien Hoa, and the coastal plain around Hoi An and Tam Ky in Quang Nam. The rent stop held at the main-season harvest of December 1964 in most districts the government controlled; landlords' agents collected under Regional Forces escort in parts of Vinh Long, Vinh Binh and Dinh Tuong, which led to the first suspension a few months later. Twenty-three team members were killed by mines and ambush during the year¹⁸⁶. The figure the Mission reported to Washington each month was titles issued; this headquarters reported it with the warning that it measured the government's paperwork, not the peasants' allegiance.

#### The dawn musters
*7 Apr – 31 Dec 1964 · ARVN 7th, 9th and 21st Divisions and the Regional and Popular Forces of the Delta, then countrywide · new · EV001-4*
The Pay and Muster Verification Service's first 20 teams began unannounced dawn musters of the ARVN 21st Division's 31st, 32nd and 33rd Regiments around Bac Lieu and Soc Trang on 7 April 1964, and of the 7th and 9th Divisions in Dinh Tuong and Vinh Long in May; by the autumn 60 teams were mustering about 480 units a month across all four corps. Each man was enrolled on an identity card with his photograph and fingerprints, and from August the Service's paymasters paid the enrolled units of IV Corps at the parade rather than through their commanders. By 31 December about 41,000 names had been struck from the rolls of a force carried at about 514,000, about 8%; the rate ran from about 4% in the Airborne and Marine battalions to about 15% in some Regional Forces companies of the Delta¹⁸⁶. These are counts of men present against names on the roll, not estimates, and they are the best figures this headquarters had ever had for the army it advised.

Washington read them as a smaller army. Secretary McNamara, visiting in May, was briefed on the first returns and told the President that the RVNAF's real strength was below its reported strength by a margin that would grow as the musters spread. In the units mustered twice, the second muster found few ghosts, because commanders had struck them before the teams came. The Commission opened 412 cases by the end of the year, almost all against company and battalion commanders and province finance officers, and brought 61 to trial¹⁸⁶.

#### The Can Giuoc killings
*9 Jun – 14 Jul 1964 · Can Giuoc and Can Duoc Districts, Long An Province · new · EV001-3*
In May 1964 titling teams from Tan An began work in the villages of Can Giuoc and Can Duoc, the rice districts of Long An between Saigon and the sea, where the survey of December 1963 had found most of the strategic hamlets destroyed and the Front's infrastructure intact. The province chief, under pressure from Saigon for a showcase close to the capital, sent the teams into hamlets the government held by day only and posted the claimants' names in the village offices, as the procedure then required¹⁸⁶. Each team worked under the escort of a Long An Regional Forces company that withdrew to its post at dusk.

On the night of 9 June armed propaganda teams of the Can Giuoc district committee, with the Long An provincial security section and screened by a company of the provincial 506th Local Force Battalion, entered three hamlets of Phuoc Lai village, read out names from the posted list, and shot four men who had claimed titles and the village land committee's secretary. Over the following five weeks the same pattern was repeated in eleven hamlets of the two districts: by 14 July 37 title holders and 9 members of village land committees had been killed, and several hundred families had brought their titles back to the district office at Can Giuoc rather than keep them in the house. The Regional Forces companies, posted in mud forts along Route 5 and the canal, did not move at night, and no reaction force came. The figures are this headquarters' count from district and province reports, cross-checked by the American province adviser, and are a floor¹⁸⁶.

The killings were the land programme's first lesson in what it did not change. Teams stopped posting names in hamlets not held at night, and titles in contested hamlets were thereafter held at the district office until the hamlet was secured or the owner asked for his. A COSVN directive captured near Duc Hoa in August told district committees not to obstruct the titling, to let the peasants "take the puppets' paper", to keep collecting the Front's agricultural tax, and to make examples only where a hamlet's people began to inform¹⁸⁶. In Can Giuoc the titling was suspended until the following spring. It was the first evidence that the reform's value in any district was exactly the value of the security behind it.

#### The battle of Nam Dong
*6 Jul 1964 · Nam Dong CIDG camp, about 50 km west of Da Nang near the Laotian border · historical · EV000-2*
At 02:30 on 6 July 1964 a force that the Americans estimated at about two battalions of Viet Cong and PAVN, some 800–900 men, attacked the CIDG camp at Nam Dong in the mountains west of Da Nang, near the Laotian border. The camp was held by Special Forces Detachment A-726 under Captain Roger H. C. Donlon, 12 Americans in all, with one Australian warrant officer of the Australian Army Training Team Vietnam, 311 Montagnard strikers, 50 Nung mercenaries and seven Vietnamese Special Forces. The attack opened with mortar fire on the command post and a sapper assault on the main gate; the outer perimeter was penetrated and many strikers deserted or went over to the attackers¹⁹⁰.

The inner perimeter, held by the Americans and the Nungs, survived about five hours of close fighting until the attackers withdrew at dawn. Two Americans and the Australian adviser were killed; 58 strikers and Nungs died and 57 were wounded, and more than 100 strikers defected. Fifty-five to sixty-two attackers' bodies were counted around the wire, a figure more reliable than most body counts because the dead lay inside a perimeter the defenders held. Donlon, wounded several times, became the first American awarded the Medal of Honor in Vietnam¹⁹⁰. Nam Dong is the pattern of the border camp war: an isolated garrison of irregulars whose loyalty is uncertain, a night assault by a force that has chosen its moment, no reinforcement before daylight, and a defence that survives on the quality of a handful of men.

#### The Gulf of Tonkin incidents and the Southeast Asia Resolution
*2–11 Aug 1964 · Gulf of Tonkin and Washington · changed from history · EV000-3*
On 30–31 July 1964 South Vietnamese commando boats under the covert OPLAN 34A programme, run by MACV's Studies and Observations Group, raided the North Vietnamese islands of Hon Me and Hon Ngu. On 2 August the destroyer USS *Maddox*, on a DESOTO signals intelligence patrol under Captain John J. Herrick, was attacked in daylight by three P-4 torpedo boats of the 135th Torpedo Squadron. The *Maddox* fired, four F-8 Crusaders from USS *Ticonderoga* attacked the retiring boats, and the North Vietnamese lost four killed and six wounded with three boats damaged; the *Maddox* was struck by one machine-gun round and suffered no casualties. Johnson, as historically, chose a warning rather than retaliation, and ordered the patrol resumed with USS *Turner Joy* in company¹⁹¹.

At Admiral Sharp's request I moved the Global Hawk orbit from the II Corps coast to the Gulf on 3 August. There were no SA-2s in the North and nothing that could reach 60,000 feet, and the aircraft took station about 40 nautical miles east of Hon Me, with the destroyers, the coast from Hon Matt to the mouth of the Song Ma and the open Gulf to the east in its radar's maritime mode⁹. On the night of 4 August the *Maddox* and *Turner Joy*, in rough weather with thunderstorms, fired for about two hours at radar contacts they took to be a second attack. The watch in the exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut that night was Major Harold Kessling's. His radar log showed the two destroyers and their manoeuvres, a cluster of fishing junks about 30 nautical miles to the west, rain cells moving across the Gulf, and no fast small craft within 40 nautical miles of the ships at any time between 2000 and 2330 local. P-4 boats making attack runs at 40 knots are good radar targets even in a sea state of three and four, and the radar had tracked the three boats of 2 August from their base on the same set. Herrick cabled that the "entire action leaves many doubts"¹⁹¹. Kessling's report reached MACV at about midnight Saigon time and CINCPAC an hour later, which in Washington was the late morning of 4 August, while McNamara and Sharp were still trying to establish what had happened¹⁸⁷.

What followed was Washington's decision, and it went the other way from history. Historically Johnson had decided on retaliation within thirty minutes of the first reports¹⁹¹. Here McNamara told him in the early afternoon that the ships' reports were contradicted by a radar that had watched the whole engagement, that Herrick himself doubted them, and that the Navy could not confirm an attack. Johnson held the strike order and, at about 1900, cancelled it. Operation Pierce Arrow was not flown. The 64 sorties against the torpedo-boat bases and the Vinh oil storage did not happen; Lieutenant (jg) Richard Sather was not killed over Hon Gai, and Lieutenant (jg) Everett Alvarez was not shot down and taken prisoner that day¹⁸⁷.

The resolution was sent anyway. Johnson had carried a draft since May, an election was three months away, and the attack of 2 August was real. On 5 August he asked Congress for authority to repel armed attack against US forces and to prevent further aggression, citing the 2 August attack on a US destroyer in international waters and Hanoi's support of the war in the South, and he said nothing of the night of the 4th. The debate ran three days longer than history's: Senator Wayne Morse, tipped about the 34A raids, asked why the destroyer had been near Hon Me at all, and Senator Gaylord Nelson's amendment against a land war was again declined by Senator Fulbright on the administration's assurance that no such war was intended. Congress passed the Southeast Asia Resolution on 10 August, 414–0 in the House and 85–3 in the Senate, in the words of the historical text¹⁸⁷. Johnson signed it on 11 August.
