On 31 March Johnson announced in a televised address that he was stopping the bombing of North Vietnam except in the area just north of the DMZ, that he would send only about 13,500 more troops, that the United States was ready to talk, and that he would not seek or accept his party's nomination for another term²⁰⁴. The speech capped the American commitment. Westmoreland's strategy of attrition by American forces had been judged by the President's own advisers to have no end that the country would accept, and from this point the questions were how to get out, and how to leave the South able to stand.

#### The My Lai massacre
*16 Mar 1968 · Son My village (My Lai 4 and My Khe), Son Tinh District, Quang Ngai Province · historical · EV000-46*
On the morning of 16 March 1968 Company C, 1st Battalion, 20th Infantry, 11th Brigade, Americal Division, operating under Task Force Barker, was landed by helicopter at the hamlet of My Lai 4 in Son My village, in an area of Quang Ngai long held by the VC 48th Local Force Battalion. It met no resistance. Its men, led among others by Second Lieutenant William L. Calley, killed the unarmed villagers they found, mostly old men, women and children, in their homes, on the paths and in an irrigation ditch; Company B, 4th Battalion, 3rd Infantry killed civilians at My Khe nearby. The dead number from 347, the US Army figure, to 504, the Vietnamese figure. Warrant Officer Hugh Thompson, flying an OH-23 scout helicopter overhead, landed between the soldiers and a group of villagers, threatened to fire on his own side, and evacuated survivors; his crewman Glenn Andreotta pulled a wounded child from the ditch²³⁹.

The Army reported the operation as a victory over 128 Viet Cong, and the brigade's investigation conceded about 20 accidental civilian deaths. The cover-up held for a year and a half, until Ronald Ridenhour's letters to Congress in March 1969 and Seymour Hersh's report of 13 November 1969²³⁹. My Lai belongs to this phase as an event of the war in the countryside: a company of a division assembled hastily, under a command climate that measured success by bodies, in a province where the population was treated as the enemy's. Its effect on the war came later, when it became known.

#### The battle of Dai Do
*30 Apr – 3 May 1968 · Dai Do and the hamlets north of Dong Ha on the Cua Viet, Quang Tri Province · historical · EV000-47*
At the end of April, as the second wave of the offensive began, battalions of the PAVN 320th Division, two of them identified as belonging to the 48th and 52nd Regiments, moved into the hamlets of Dai Do, Dinh To and their neighbours north of Dong Ha, threatening the Marine base and the Cua Viet supply route. The 2nd Battalion, 4th Marines under Lieutenant Colonel William Weise attacked across open paddies from amphibian tractors and boats, with the 1st Battalion, 3rd Marines, ARVN units of the 2nd Regiment and a mechanized battalion, and from 1 May the US 3rd Battalion, 21st Infantry. The Marines lost 81 killed and the ARVN 5; the Americans claimed more than 600 PAVN killed, and the PAVN has not released its losses. By 3 May the PAVN had gone. Whether Dai Do was a main thrust against Dong Ha or a diversion is not settled; the frontal assaults of 1 and 2 May were costly enough to be criticized by later historians²⁴⁰.

#### The Paris talks open
*May 1968 · Paris · historical · EV000-48*
After weeks of argument over a site, talks between the United States and North Vietnam opened in Paris in May 1968, with W. Averell Harriman leading for the United States and Xuan Thuy for Hanoi. They stalled at once: Hanoi demanded a complete and unconditional end to the bombing of the North before substantive talks, and refused to recognise the Saigon government; Saigon refused to sit with the Front²⁴¹. Through the summer the talks were a forum for statements, while both sides fought for position on the ground. Their opening nonetheless changed the war's logic. From May 1968 both governments were fighting to shape a settlement as well as to win, and every offensive that followed had a negotiating purpose.

#### The May offensive in Saigon
*5 May – 15 Jun 1968 · Saigon and its outskirts, and towns throughout the South · historical · EV000-49*
On 5 May the PAVN and VC launched the second phase of the general offensive, which the Americans called "mini-Tet", with attacks by fire on more than 100 towns and installations and a renewed assault on Saigon. VC battalions fought their way into the southern and western districts of the capital and into Cholon, where they were destroyed or driven out by the ARVN and US forces over several weeks, with air strikes and artillery used inside the city; much of District 8 was destroyed and tens of thousands were made homeless. The phase lasted until 15 June²³³. Its losses fell heavily on the same local forces and cadres who had survived February, and many of the units that fought in Saigon in May were filled out with northern replacements. American deaths in May 1968 were the highest of any month of the war.

#### The evacuation of Kham Duc
*10–12 May 1968 · Kham Duc Special Forces camp and Ngok Tavak, Quang Tin Province · historical · EV000-50*
On 10 May the 40th Battalion of the PAVN 2nd Division's 1st Regiment overran the outpost of Ngok Tavak, held by an Australian-led company of the 11th MIKE Force and a battery of the 2nd Battalion, 13th Marines. The PAVN 1st and 21st Regiments then closed on the camp at Kham Duc, held by Special Forces Detachment A-105, CIDG and a SOG detachment, reinforced by the 2nd Battalion, 1st Infantry of the Americal Division. On 11 May Westmoreland approved evacuation, and on 12 May, under about 350 air sorties, roughly 1,500 soldiers and civilians were flown out. A C-130 flown by Major Bernard L. Bucher was shot down just after take-off, killing all 183 Vietnamese civilians aboard, a Special Forces officer and the five crewmen; a second C-130 was wrecked on the runway. Lieutenant Colonel Joe M. Jackson landed a C-123 on the abandoned strip to recover a three-man combat control team left behind, and received the Medal of Honor. The Americans lost about 42–46 killed and 31–33 missing and twelve aircraft; about 220 CIDG were killed or missing; the US claimed about 345 PAVN killed, and a later study estimates 1,500–2,000. The abandonment of the camp removed one of the few allied positions between the Laotian border and the lowlands of Quang Tin and Quang Nam, and its loss was one of the few clear PAVN victories over a US-defended position in 1968²⁴².

#### ◆ Abrams takes command; mobilization in the South; Khe Sanh abandoned
*10 Jun – 11 Jul 1968 · Saigon and Quang Tri Province · changed from history · EV000-51*
On 10 June 1968 General Creighton W. Abrams, Westmoreland's deputy since May 1967, succeeded him as COMUSMACV; Westmoreland went home to be Army Chief of Staff. Abrams spoke of "one war", in which the main-force battle, pacification and the improvement of the RVNAF were parts of one campaign rather than separate efforts, and gave more weight to the enemy's logistics system, to territorial security and to the South Vietnamese forces. How much changed in practice is disputed: large operations continued into 1969 and his own strategy papers did not announce a new strategy²⁴³.

In mid-June the National Assembly in Saigon passed the general mobilization law the government had been unable to pass before Tet, inducting men aged 18 to 38 and ordering youths of 17 and men of 39 to 43 into civil defence; about 220,000 men were inducted before the summer ended. RVNAF strength rose from about 643,000 in 1967 to about 897,000 in 1969²⁴⁴. Historically the expansion outran the rifles: the M16 went first to the regular divisions in 1968, and the Regional and Popular Forces raised that summer drew carbines and Garands and waited until 1969–70 for anything better. In this war the new RF companies and PF platoons drew M4A1s from the 1968 increment, about 260,000 carbines already in the depots at Long Binh and Da Nang by June against the year's expected growth, and were on the ranges within a month of induction. The constraint on the expansion was not rifles but sergeants: the training centres could zero a carbine in an afternoon and could not make a squad leader in less than a year, and Venn's successor reported in September that about a third of the new PF platoons had no one who had held the rank before. The old weapons the RVNAF had handed in since 1964 went, with the Garands the Combat Youth already held, to the People's Self-Defense Force formed under the same law¹⁸⁴.

On 19 June the Marines began evacuating Khe Sanh, which Westmoreland had held through the siege; the base was closed on 5 July and the last Marines left the area on 11 July²³⁷. The decision was explained as the product of more helicopters and a mobile defence of the DMZ, but it was read by both sides as an admission of what the base had cost.

#### The third offensive
*17 Aug – 23 Sep 1968 · Provincial towns and bases, especially in III Corps and the Central Highlands · changed from history · EV000-52*
The third phase of the general offensive began in mid-August with attacks by fire and ground assaults on towns and bases, including attacks around Tay Ninh in III Corps. It was weaker than its predecessors: the local forces that had carried the attacks of February and May were largely gone, and the main forces would not commit themselves against American firepower in the same way again. It ended in late September without lasting gains. Taken together, the three offensives of 1968 cost the VC about 50,000 dead by one estimate against about 4,000 US and 6,000 ARVN. Their recruitment in the South collapsed: in Region 5, 1,700 recruits in 1969 against 8,000 in 1968, in the Mekong lowlands 100 against 16,000. By the end of the year about 70 percent of the main-force troops in the South are reported to have been northerners. MACV estimated total PAVN and VC strength at 287,000 in January 1968 and 251,000 at its end²³³. The losses forced a reassessment in Hanoi that would carry into 1969.

In this war the third offensive runs as it did historically, in a part of the country the provisions barely touched. What changes is the arithmetic behind it. The Front's recruitment in the Mekong lowlands in 1968, 16,000 on the historical course, is about 11,000 here, because about a third of the tenant families who would have supplied the recruits had been titled by the government in districts it held at night, and the Front's own conscription in those districts had become coercion that the PF platoons could resist. The 1969 collapse comes from a lower base and is no less complete. The consequence is that COSVN begins filling its Delta main-force battalions with northern replacements in the summer of 1968, several months earlier than on the historical course, and the northerners who arrive in Chuong Thien and Kien Giang are strangers in hamlets where the Front's strength had always been that its soldiers were local. The proportion of northerners in the main forces at the year's end, about 70 percent historically²³³, is a few points higher here¹⁸⁶.

#### The bombing halt and the American election
*31 Oct – 5 Nov 1968 · Washington, Paris and Saigon · historical · EV000-53*
In October Hanoi signalled that it would accept the Saigon government at the table if the bombing stopped. On 31 October Johnson announced the end of all bombing of North Vietnam from 1 November, ending Rolling Thunder²⁰⁴. Thieu refused to join the talks on the terms agreed. Through Anna Chennault the Nixon campaign had encouraged him to wait for a better deal, and on 2 November she told the South Vietnamese ambassador to "hold on a while longer"; Johnson learned of it through intercepts and ordered the campaign watched, but did not make it public. Historians disagree on whether the channel changed anything, since Thieu had his own reasons to refuse. On 5 November Richard M. Nixon was elected president by a narrow popular margin. Talks in Paris did not resume in substance until the new year, after a dispute over the shape of the table was settled with a round table for the governments²⁴¹.

Over three years and eight months Rolling Thunder had flown 153,784 USAF and 152,399 Navy and Marine attack sorties, and lost 506 USAF, 397 Navy and 19 Marine aircraft over or near the North²⁰⁴. It ended without having achieved its purposes of breaking Hanoi's will or stopping the flow of men and supplies south.

#### The Accelerated Pacification Campaign and Phoenix
*1 Nov 1968 – Jan 1969 · The countryside of South Vietnam · changed from history · EV000-54*
Phung Hoang, the Vietnamese programme to coordinate the attack on the VC infrastructure, had been established by decree in December 1967 out of the American ICEX programme of June 1967, and Thieu put his own decree behind it in July 1968. Its instruments were district intelligence centres, the CIA-run Provincial Reconnaissance Units and the police; its method was to identify, capture, turn or kill the cadres of the parallel administration²⁴⁵. On 1 November 1968, at the urging of Komer and his successor William E. Colby, the government launched the Accelerated Pacification Campaign, a three-month drive to raise about 1,000 contested hamlets to relative security before any ceasefire could freeze the map, using RF and PF, Revolutionary Development cadre and the new People's Self-Defence Force, with regular forces screening.

The campaign met its targets by the Hamlet Evaluation System, a scale filled in by American district advisers under pressure to show progress, and in many places those hamlets did become more secure after dark, because the VC local forces that had protected the infrastructure had died in the offensives. Phoenix's own figures, 81,740 people "neutralized" between 1968 and 1972, 26,369 of them killed, were produced against monthly quotas that encouraged false arrests and padding, and about 87 percent of the killings credited to it were by ordinary military operations. Communist cadres later described 1968–72 as their hardest period, and some American advisers concluded that Phoenix had been a mortal threat in some provinces and a paper programme in others²⁴⁵.

In this war the campaign and Phoenix are launched on the historical dates, under the same men, with the same quotas. Three things are different. The district intelligence centres start from the land register, which by November 1968 records about 900,000 titled hectares and the households that hold them; for the first time a district knew who lived in each titled hamlet, and who had left it for the Front, from a record compiled for another purpose and not for the quota. And from December the Commission Against Corruption's inspectors, under an agreement Colby made with Commissioner Khoa, sampled the neutralization reports of 40 districts in IV and III Corps each quarter and checked each name against the register, the province prison rolls and the PRU and police reports. The first sample, reported in February 1969, found about a third of the claimed neutralizations unsupported: names that appeared on no record, persons arrested for debt or feud, and killings in ordinary operations credited to Phoenix. The quotas were not abolished, but in the sampled districts the padding fell, false arrests for ransom became cases before the Special Court, and the district chiefs learned that a name would be checked. The register had been made public in 1964 only as parcel numbers, after Can Giuoc; in 1968 it was used, for the first time, as a list of persons, and the Front's propaganda said so¹⁸⁶.

The third is that Phung Hoang was not a new instrument here but a new name over an old one. The district intelligence and operations coordinating centres had sat in the Special Branch's district offices since ICEX in 1967, and the Special Branch had been running target folders in the held belt since 1964. The Commission's sample of February 1969 found about a third of the claimed neutralizations unsupported across the 40 districts; in the districts where a Special Branch section three or more years old ran the folders, the unsupported share was about a tenth, and in those where the Provincial Reconnaissance Units and the sector S2 ran them, about a half. The quotas fell hardest where the institution was youngest, which was in the contested belt and the districts recovered after Tet, and there Phoenix was the paper programme and the instrument of feud its critics described¹⁶⁵. In the held belt it was a refinement of what the police had done for four years, and the Front's cadres there had left before it began.

#### The halt measured
*1 Nov – 31 Dec 1968 · The coastal road and railway from Thanh Hoa to Vinh and Dong Hoi, Route 15 to the Mu Gia pass, and the ferries at Quang Khe, North Vietnam · new · EV003-5*
The bombing of the North stopped on 1 November 1968. The Gulf watch did not. Its orbit, 40–60 nautical miles off the coast and outside the SA-2 belt, was a reconnaissance flight over international waters, and it went on counting the traffic on Route 1A, the coastal railway and Route 15 below the 19th parallel through November and December⁹. What it saw changed within a week. Trucks that had moved only at night and in rain, in convoys of five or six, moved by day in columns of forty and fifty; the ferries at Quang Khe ran in daylight; the railway, repaired south of Thanh Hoa within three weeks, carried trains to Vinh. Southbound vehicle movements on Route 15 toward the Mu Gia pass in December were about three times the October level¹⁸⁷.

The count went to Washington twice a week, as the bombing halt's terms required this command to report the North's use of it. It did not change the halt, which the President had traded for the opening of the expanded talks in Paris and which the President-elect did not reverse. It changed the next campaign. When Commando Hunt opened on 15 November, its planners knew from the radar how much more was coming toward the passes, and when and where, and the U-2S found the new truck parks and the new fuel dumps north of Mu Gia that the surge required¹⁸⁷. The halt was Hanoi's to use, and it used it exactly as expected; the difference was that the use was measured as it happened, and the headquarters that would fight the dry season of 1968–69 knew the size of it on its first night.

#### Commando Hunt begins
*15 Nov 1968 · The Laotian panhandle · changed from history · EV000-55*
On 15 November, two weeks after the bombing of the North stopped, the Seventh Air Force opened Commando Hunt, a campaign against the Trail in southern Laos fought in seasonal phases: dry-season phases against the trucks moving south, wet-season phases against the roads. Aircraft freed from the North were moved to it. It was the first campaign built around Igloo White: sensors seeded along the roads reported to the Infiltration Surveillance Center at Nakhon Phanom, whose computers passed target coordinates to strike aircraft, and gunships, above all the AC-130 Spectre, hunted trucks at night²⁴⁶. In this war the Global Hawk's panhandle watch was the campaign's third sensor from its first night: it saw the trucks in the open where the sensors could not hear them, and the AC-130s of the 16th Special Operations Squadron were sent to radar tracks as well as to sensor activations¹⁸⁷.

Phase I, to 20 April 1969, claimed 7,322 trucks destroyed at a cost of 56 allied aircraft to an estimated 600 anti-aircraft weapons. The truck counts were not believed by the CIA, which estimated the North's entire truck inventory at about 6,000²⁴⁶. Here they were not believed by this headquarters either. The radar counted the trucks entering the panhandle at Mu Gia and Ban Karai and leaving it toward the South on Routes 92 and 96, and the difference between what went in and what came out, less what the radar saw stop and not move again, was the measure of what the bombing destroyed. By that measure Phase I destroyed or disabled about 2,600–3,200 trucks and reduced the tonnage reaching the South by about a sixth, not the third the claims implied. MACV J-2 sent that figure to Washington in May 1969 beside the Air Force's, and from then on the two numbers travelled together¹⁸⁷. Commando Hunt would run until March 1972, and the Trail would grow under it; this time the growth was counted.

#### How the phase ends
At the end of 1968 the war has changed shape without changing direction. The VC in the South have lost much of their main and local forces and many of their cadres in the three offensives, and their recruitment has collapsed; the main-force war is now predominantly a PAVN war, and the parallel administration in the hamlets, though intact in much of the country, is under more pressure than at any time since 1959. The government in Saigon has survived Tet, mobilized, and begun with CORDS to push into the countryside; ARVN units fought better in February than anyone expected, though they remain dependent on American firepower and logistics. Against these gains, the United States has decided not to escalate further and to seek a negotiated way out; the bombing of the North has ended; and the American public's tolerance for casualties, measured in a year in which 14,592 Americans died by hostile action, has been spent¹⁸⁵.

In Hanoi, the leadership has paid heavily for the General Offensive and turned back to protracted war for the moment, but has won what it most needed: an American president who will not reinforce and a successor committed to withdrawal. The Paris talks exist; the shape of the table is being settled. Phase IV opens with a new administration in Washington, a war in the countryside that the South is better placed to fight than it has been for years, and an American army whose withdrawal is now a matter of timing.

In this war the countryside is better placed than that. About 900,000 hectares have been titled, the Front's Delta recruitment has fallen from a lower base, and its Delta battalions are filling with northerners earlier¹⁸⁶. The American decision of 1968 is unchanged, and so is its timing.

The National Police numbers about 95,000 and the Special Branch has buried its first constables of the Tet battles; the infrastructure in the held belt is weaker than anywhere since 1959, and every file it keeps is now held under a destruction plan¹⁶⁵.

The enemy's rear enters Phase IV counted: the surge after the halt is measured, the first dry season of Commando Hunt is scored against the radar as well as the claims¹⁸⁷, and the American decision of 1968 is unchanged by any of it.

The RVNAF ends 1968 larger and armed throughout with weapons the historical one was still waiting for; its new territorials have rifles and too few sergeants¹⁸⁴. The gap the rearmament opened over history is now closing from the historical side, as the M16 reaches the ARVN in 1968 and the territorials in 1969.

### Mechanism

Tet is where four years of rearmament were tested against the enemy's whole local-force structure in one night, and the result was the one Part 2 predicted for the outposts, scaled up. The cities went as they went historically, because main-force battalions and PAVN regiments fighting block by block are not decided by the infantry's rifles. The district towns did not. The first wave in the districts was made by local-force battalions and companies against an RF company or two behind a compound wall, and about a third of those attacks never got inside. Cai Lay is the case: the 311th RF Company held the district compound against a battalion of the 261st through the night, and the battalion that had planned to be through the town by four was still in the market when the 7th Division arrived. Historically the territorials met Tet with Korean War weapons and the ARVN with the Garand, and the M16 airlift to the ARVN was one of the first decisions after it.

At Hue the provision lowered the cost and not the length. The Marines' marksmen killed PAVN machine gunners in the windows of the treasury from two hundred metres; the 106mm recoilless rifles, the Ontos and the weather decided the pace. About forty fewer ARVN soldiers and thirty-five fewer Americans died there than historically.

The mobilization of June 1968 shows the bottleneck migrating once more. There were rifles for every new territorial within the month. There were not sergeants, and a third of the new PF platoons had no one who had held the rank before. The rifles had removed the constraint the historical expansion faced and exposed the one beneath it.

### Enemy response and initiative

The enemy's response at Tet was the plan he had already made, and it was the purge, not anything we did, that made it a plan against an ARVN he had misread. After the first wave the Front's regional commands drew the right conclusion faster than Hanoi did: the May and August offensives in the Delta were made against outposts and towns with mortars and rockets from outside the wire, not with assault companies against the walls, and the Front's local forces in IV Corps spent the summer rebuilding rather than attacking¹⁸⁴. Its rearming from our stocks went on; by the end of 1968 most of its local-force companies in the Delta carried at least some captured M4A1s, and it bought their ammunition in the towns.

### Causal chains

**C004-4** [months] The Directorate issues about a third of the old weapons the RVNAF handed in to the National Police, the Combat Youth and the militia in 1964, over this headquarters' objection → those weapons become the Front's easiest source of rifles in the held belt in 1965 → after Tet, with the territorials armed from stock, the remaining Garands and carbines go to the People's Self-Defense Force formed under the mobilization law → the self-defence militia of 1968–69, historically armed with whatever the regular forces had discarded, carries in this war the same rifles that the Front had already learned to take from hamlet militia in 1965, so the PSDF begins its life as a known target with a known yield: the cost of a 1964 decision about surplus weapons is paid again by a new force four years later.

### Downstream

From 1969 the historical ARVN catches up. The M16 reaches the regular divisions in 1968 and the territorials in 1969, and the gap this provision opened over history narrows to the squad gun, the marksman and the practice, which is why Phase IV and Phase V are stated nulls. The PSDF begins as a target. The Front's local forces in the Delta, gutted at the district walls on the first night, have less to rebuild with than historically, and the government's move into the countryside in the autumn finds the districts already emptied of them.

### Clock

The provision's effect on the American clock in 1968 is real, small and invisible in the decision that mattered. About thirty-five fewer Americans died at Hue and fewer in the first minutes of every contact, and there was no M16 scandal in the summer of 1967 to add a story of official failure to the progress campaign's collapse. None of it moved the decision of 31 March, which was taken on the same day for the same reasons: the reinforcement request, the progress campaign's failure, and 14,592 American dead by hostile action in the year on the historical course, a figure the rifles lower by a few hundred at most¹⁸⁴. The clock runs on the same dates.

## §4.P4 Phase IV — Vietnamization and drawdown (Jan 1969 – Dec 1971)

The rearmament does not bite in Phase IV because the historical army catches up with it here. The ARVN's better end to 1968 does not carry forward as a separate advantage, because by 1969 the historical ARVN carries the M16 and from 1969–70 its territorials do too. What remains is the squad gun, the marksman and four years' practice. The incursion, Lam Son 719 and the drawdown were decided by the frontier constraints, by command failures at the landing zones, and by Washington's timetable, none of which a squad weapon touches. The rifles remain present and leave the phase's course unchanged.

## §4.P5 Phase V — The Easter Offensive and Linebacker (Jan 1972 – Dec 1972)

The Easter Offensive is decided by armour, artillery, B-52s and the quality of corps command, and the provision touches none of them. Quang Tri falls in May because its defence collapses at the top; An Loc and Kontum hold because the bombers hold them. A belt-fed gun in each squad at An Loc made every assault on the perimeter dearer, and the PAVN answered with 130mm guns, which no rifle reaches. By 1972 the historical ARVN carries the M16 and the M60, and the gap left is too narrow to change any battle's result.

## §4.P6 Phase VI — Paris, the endgame, and 1975 (Jan 1973 – Dec 1975)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: as historical on every headline variable.*

Phase VI is the war without the Americans, and the end of the Republic of Vietnam. The Paris Agreement of January 1973 takes US forces out and returns the prisoners; it leaves the PAVN in the South, the Thieu government in Saigon, and the war to be decided between them. The ceasefire never holds. In 1973 Saigon is on the offensive, recovering hamlets and roads, while the North rebuilds; in 1974 the two are evenly matched, and the ARVN, short of ammunition, fuel and spare parts as American aid falls, bleeds in a war of attrition it cannot sustain; in January 1975 the North tests whether the United States will respond to the seizure of a province, and when it does not, it commits everything²⁴⁷.

The shape of the phase is three slow years and seven fast weeks. The constraints that bind the American role are now law, not policy: the Case–Church amendment ends all US combat activity in Indochina from 15 August 1973, the War Powers Resolution limits the President's power to send forces back, and Congress cuts military aid. Watergate removes the President who gave Thieu his assurances. In Hanoi, the 21st Plenum of October 1973 returns to revolutionary violence, and the PAVN builds a road network east of the mountains, a fuel pipeline into the South and an army of corps. In Saigon, the economy, hit by the oil shock and the departure of the Americans whose spending had sustained it, and a government that has narrowed its base, face the offensive of 1975 with an army spread thin across the whole country. When the blow falls at Ban Me Thuot in March, Thieu's decision to abandon the Highlands turns a defeat into a rout, and in eight weeks the Republic falls. The PAVN enters Saigon on 30 April 1975.

In this war the army that fights the last three years has been built for ten years around the belt-fed gun and the abundance of American ammunition, and the ration of 1974 finds it more dependent on volume than the historical one. It fights the attrition of 1974 and the stand at Xuan Loc with better weapons than its enemy and too few rounds to use them, and the hoards it builds against the ration are lost with I Corps.

#### The Paris Agreement
*27 Jan 1973 · Paris · historical · EV000-83*
On 27 January 1973 the Agreement on Ending the War and Restoring Peace in Vietnam was signed in Paris by the United States, the Democratic Republic of Vietnam, the Republic of Vietnam and the Provisional Revolutionary Government. A ceasefire in place took effect on 28 January Saigon time. The United States and its allies would withdraw all forces within 60 days, and prisoners would be exchanged in the same period. Nothing required the PAVN to leave the South, where by US estimates about 150,000 of its troops remained. The Thieu government remained, and a National Council of National Reconciliation and Concord was to arrange elections, which it never did. An International Commission of Control and Supervision of Canadian, Hungarian, Indonesian and Polish personnel, and a Four-Party Joint Military Commission, were to supervise the ceasefire, and both sides could replace worn-out armaments one for one. Thieu signed under American pressure, with Nixon's written promises of continued aid and of a forceful response to a major violation²⁴¹. The agreement was close to the terms of October 1972.

#### The ceasefire that never held
*28 Jan – Dec 1973 · The countryside of South Vietnam · historical · EV000-84*
In the last days before the ceasefire both sides grabbed for hamlets and roads, raising flags to claim territory, the "war of the flags"; the ARVN recovered most of what the PAVN and VC had seized. Fighting resumed in earnest by March²⁴¹. Through most of 1973 the Saigon government was on the offensive: the Communists, rebuilding supply lines and wary of provoking the return of American air power, largely stayed on the defensive, while the ARVN and territorial forces pushed into PRG-held areas. Both sides violated the agreement from the start; the ICCS could not stop it, and Canada left the commission in July 1973. From late 1973 the PAVN grew bolder as the American political system turned on itself, and by early 1974 the two sides fought roughly on equal terms²⁴⁷.

#### Operation Homecoming and the last American troops
*12 Feb – 29 Mar 1973 · Hanoi, Clark Air Base and Saigon · historical · EV000-85*
On 12 February 1973 the first American prisoners were flown out of Hanoi to Clark Air Base in the Philippines. By 29 March, 566 US servicemen, 23 American civilians and 5 foreign nationals had come home through Clark²⁴⁸. On 29 March 1973 the last US combat and support troops left South Vietnam, and MACV was disestablished. A Defense Attaché Office under Major General John E. Murray, with a few dozen military personnel and civilian contractors, took over the management of American military aid. The United States still maintained air power in Thailand and the Seventh Fleet offshore, and continued to bomb in Cambodia. The question of Americans missing in action, about whom Hanoi gave little information, would keep the war alive in American politics for two decades.

#### Case–Church and the end of American combat in Indochina
*Jun – 7 Nov 1973 · Washington; Cambodia · historical · EV000-86*
In June 1973 Congress passed the Case–Church amendment, which barred any US military activity in or over Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia without congressional approval from 15 August 1973²⁴¹. On 15 August the bombing of Cambodia, which had continued since the Paris Agreement, ended. On 7 November Congress passed the War Powers Resolution over Nixon's veto, limiting the President's power to commit forces to hostilities without Congress. Nixon's promise to Thieu of a forceful response to a major violation was now beyond the President's power to keep without Congress, and Congress would not give it. The sanctuary constraint, which had shaped the war since 1964, now covered the whole of Indochina and was written into law.

#### Resolution 21 and the North's rebuilding
*Oct 1973 – 1974 · Hanoi; the Laotian panhandle and the western Highlands · historical · EV000-87*
In October 1973 the 21st Plenum of the Party Central Committee in Hanoi resolved that the revolution in the South must continue by the path of revolutionary violence, and that the PAVN should respond to Saigon's operations and prepare for a decisive offensive. The PAVN rebuilt through 1973 and 1974: it improved the strategic road along the western side of South Vietnam east of the Truong Son, extended the fuel pipeline into the South, built up stocks of ammunition and armour in the base areas and grouped its divisions into army corps, the 1st Corps in October 1973 and the 4th Corps on 20 July 1974 among them²⁴⁹. It was the North's own decision, on its own reading of the American political collapse and Saigon's weakness, and it was taken while Saigon still held the initiative in the countryside.

#### Saigon declares the war resumed; the Paracels
*Jan 1974 · Saigon; the Paracel Islands · historical · EV000-88*
In January 1974 Thieu announced that the war had resumed and that the Paris Agreement no longer bound his forces²⁴¹. On 19 January a Chinese naval force defeated South Vietnamese ships in the Paracel Islands, and China took control of the islands²⁵⁰. The United States did not intervene. The clash showed how isolated Saigon had become: its ally would not act, and a great power on its northern horizon was willing to take territory from it.

#### ◆ The aid cuts and the shortages
*25 Jan – 9 Aug 1974 · South Vietnam and Washington · changed from history · EV000-89*
On 25 January 1974 supplies of ammunition and fuel to all ARVN units were cut because of budget limits. US military aid fell from about $1.1 billion in fiscal year 1974 to an appropriation of about $700 million for fiscal year 1975, the July 1974–June 1975 year, the figure given by source as $700–750 million²⁴⁷. Major General Murray of the Defense Attaché Office warned that cuts in support could roughly be equated with loss of territory, and that aid of $600 million would mean writing the South off²⁵⁰. The ARVN rationed artillery rounds, grounded aircraft and helicopters for lack of fuel and parts, and cut patrols; the soldiers' pay lost most of its value to inflation, and desertion rose. The economy suffered from the world oil shock of late 1973 and the loss of American spending.

In this war the ration fell on an army that had been built for ten years around the belt-fed gun and the 30-round magazine, four years longer than the historical one. The Joint General Staff's directive of February 1974 limited each infantry squad to its basic load for a month's operations, and the squad gun went first: about 200 rounds a gun for a month, which is what an M249 fires at the rapid rate in two minutes. Battalion commanders who had learned their trade breaking contacts with the squad gun's volume found that the 1974 ration let them do it about twice in a month, and the advisers' successors at the Defense Attaché Office reported that the infantry, rather than fight with aimed rifle fire as their fathers had fought with the Garand, avoided contacts it could not break with volume. The marksmen, whose match 7.62mm had a separate line, were rationed harder still; by the summer about half the M110A2s in I and II Corps were in arms rooms for want of ammunition. Divisions began to hoard linked 5.56mm in their own ammunition points against the ration, against JGS orders, and by the end of the year the 1st, 2nd and 3rd Divisions held about 9 million rounds outside the corps depots between them¹⁸⁴. On 9 August 1974 Nixon resigned over Watergate, and Gerald Ford succeeded him²⁵⁰. The President who had given Thieu written promises was gone.

#### The ARVN's last offensives
*12 Feb – 2 May 1974 · Tri Phap, Dinh Tuong Province; Svay Rieng, Cambodia · historical · EV000-90*
In the spring of 1974 the ARVN still had the strength to attack. From 12 February to 4 May it struck the PAVN base at Tri Phap in Dinh Tuong Province in the Delta. From 27 March to 2 May, in the last major ARVN offensive of the war, III Corps attacked the PAVN base areas around Svay Rieng in Cambodia, claiming more than 1,200 PAVN killed against fewer than 100 ARVN²⁵⁰. They were the work of an army still able to win local battles when it had the ammunition and air support to do so. They did not change the balance, and after the spring the ARVN was on the defensive everywhere.

#### ◆ The war of attrition of 1974
*16 May – 10 Dec 1974 · The Iron Triangle; Thuong Duc, Duc Duc and Phu Loc in I Corps · changed from history · EV000-91*
From May 1974 the PAVN took the initiative with a series of "strategic raids" designed to grind down the ARVN and test its reserves. In III Corps the PAVN 9th Division attacked Rach Bap in the Iron Triangle from 16 May; the ARVN retook it only in November. In I Corps a PAVN regiment overran An Hoa and attacked the district town of Thuong Duc from 18 July; the Airborne Division held the heights around it in a costly victory that lasted until November. At Duc Duc, from 18 July to 4 October, the ARVN 3rd Division suffered 4,700 casualties. From 28 August to 10 December the PAVN seized hills near Phu Loc that closed Phu Bai airfield to traffic and cut Highway 1, and the ARVN recovered them at heavy cost. In 1974 South Vietnam lost about 30,000 killed; the US estimated about 61,000 PAVN and VC killed²⁵⁰.

The weapons the ARVN carried in these fights were better than the PAVN's, as they had been since 1964, and it mattered less than at any time since. On the ridges west of Thuong Duc in September the 1st and 2nd Airborne Brigades took and retook Hill 1062 against the PAVN 304th Division's 66th Regiment; the Airborne's marksmen, fed from the brigade's hoarded match ammunition, killed the 66th's spotters and gunners on the facing slopes at 500–700 metres, and its squads held the crest against three night assaults with the squad guns rationed to a belt each for the night. They held, and they bled, because the PAVN's 130mm guns and 122mm rockets were not rationed and the ARVN's 105s and 155s were. At Duc Duc the 3rd Division, whose squads had been trained since 1966 to fix an enemy with the squad gun and call the artillery, found that it had neither belts nor shells enough to do either and fought a war of aimed rifle fire it had never practised; I keep its losses at the historical 4,700 because I cannot show they were larger, only that the division was less able to fight the war it was given¹⁸⁴. I keep South Vietnam's dead for the year at about 30,000²⁵⁰. The ARVN, unable to replace its losses or its expended ammunition, had committed its strategic reserve, the Airborne and Marine Divisions, to static defence in I Corps, and had nothing left to move.

#### The fall of Phuoc Long
*12 Dec 1974 – 6 Jan 1975 · Phuoc Long Province (Phuoc Binh and Song Be), III Corps · historical · EV000-92*
On 12 December 1974 the PAVN 4th Corps under Hoang Cam, with the 7th Division, the new 3rd Division and independent regiments including the 141st, 165th, 201st and 271st, and two armoured companies with 14 T-54s, attacked Phuoc Long Province on the Cambodian border. It was defended mainly by Regional Force battalions, reinforced by the 1st and 2nd Battalions, 7th Regiment and the 1st Battalion, 9th Regiment of the 5th Division and the 81st Airborne Ranger Battalion. The last ARVN post fell on 6 January 1975, the first provincial capital lost permanently since the Paris Agreement. The ARVN lost more than 1,000 killed and 2,444 captured; the PAVN about 1,300 killed and wounded. Saigon did not try to retake it. Washington made no military response²⁴⁹. In Hanoi, where a strategy conference sat from 18 December to 8 January, Phuoc Long answered the question the leadership had set itself: the United States would not return²⁵⁰. The plan for a two-year campaign to end the war in 1976 was adapted to strike in the Central Highlands in 1975.

#### The fall of Ban Me Thuot
*10–12 Mar 1975 · Ban Me Thuot, Darlac Province · historical · EV000-93*
For Campaign 275 the PAVN Central Highlands Front under Lieutenant General Hoang Minh Thao, directed by the chief of the general staff, General Van Tien Dung, massed the 10th, 316th and 320A Divisions with the 3rd and 968th Divisions, tanks, artillery and anti-aircraft units. Diversionary attacks on Pleiku and Kontum, false radio traffic and local rumours drew the attention of the II Corps commander, Major General Pham Van Phu, who discounted repeated warnings and kept his forces in the north. At 02:00 on 10 March the PAVN attacked Ban Me Thuot, defended mainly by the 53rd Regiment of the 23rd Division with Rangers and Regional Forces; the town was in PAVN hands by 11–12 March. The counter-attack by the rest of the 23rd Division, lifted in by helicopter, was broken. The PAVN admitted 600–800 killed and 2,416 wounded; the Vietnamese official history counts 28,514 ARVN put out of action, 4,502 killed and 16,822 captured. II Corps lost more than three-quarters of its combat units²⁵¹. Ban Me Thuot was the strategic surprise of the war's last campaign.

#### The collapse of I Corps
*13–30 Mar 1975 · Quang Tri, Hue and Da Nang · historical · EV000-94*
On 13 March Thieu ordered Truong to give up most of I Corps and withdrew the Airborne Division towards Saigon as a reserve. On 20 March he ordered Truong to hold only Da Nang; on 29 March he ordered him to turn back and retake it, which Truong refused. The contradictory orders, given as the PAVN 2nd Corps with the 304th, 324th and 325th Divisions attacked from the north and west and the 2nd Division and 52nd Brigade struck in Quang Ngai and Quang Tin, broke I Corps. Northern Quang Tri was lost by 18 March; Hue was surrounded by the evening of 25 March and abandoned; about 6,000 soldiers of the 2nd Division deserted on 28 March; and the PAVN entered Da Nang on the afternoon of 29 March. Soldiers abandoned their units to find their families, and the sea evacuation from Da Nang became chaos, with people fighting to board the ships. I Corps had about 134,000 men against more than 75,000 PAVN and VC; three ARVN infantry divisions and the 147th Marine Brigade ceased to exist, and about 58,722 soldiers were captured at Hue. The PAVN took 129 aircraft, 179 armoured vehicles and 327 artillery pieces²⁵². The best corps in the ARVN, under its best commander, had been destroyed without a decisive battle.

#### The retreat from the Highlands
*14–25 Mar 1975 · Pleiku and Kontum to the coast along Route 7, through Cheo Reo · historical · EV000-95*
On 14 March at Cam Ranh, Thieu ordered Phu to abandon Pleiku and Kontum and pull II Corps back to the coast to defend the populated regions. The chief of the Joint General Staff, General Cao Van Vien, warned against Route 19 because of the French disaster at the Mang Yang Pass in 1954; Thieu chose Route 7, a disused road in poor condition. The retreat was ordered verbally and kept secret, even from many of the units and the province chiefs; within days some 400,000 civilians had joined the columns. The PAVN ambushed the column near Cheo Reo, where fighting ended on 19 March, and shelled it along the road. Only the 6th Ranger Battalion and the 19th Armoured Cavalry Squadron arrived in good order; an estimated 155,000 civilians were killed or abducted on the "convoy of tears", and RVNAF air strikes hit civilians too²⁵¹. It was the decision that lost the war in the shortest time: II Corps, which had held the Highlands through every offensive since 1965, was destroyed on the road in ten days.

#### ◆ Xuan Loc
*9–21 Apr 1975 · Xuan Loc, Long Khanh Province, northeast of Saigon · changed from history · EV000-96*
On 9 April the PAVN 4th Corps under Major General Hoang Cam, with the 6th, 7th and 341st Divisions and tanks and artillery, attacked Xuan Loc, the road junction northeast of Saigon on Route 1. The town was held by the ARVN 18th Division under Brigadier General Le Minh Dao, with the 1st Airborne Brigade, armoured units, Rangers and Regional Forces, and air support from Bien Hoa and Tan Son Nhut. The 18th Division held for twelve days against repeated assaults; on 12 April two BLU-82 bombs were dropped on PAVN troops, reportedly killing about 200²⁵³.

In this war the 18th Division fought at Xuan Loc with what was left of a decade's equipment and, by April, with the ammunition the Joint General Staff could send it from the Long Binh depot, which by then was most of what remained in III Corps. Its squads had their squad guns and the belts to feed them for the first time since January 1974, because Dao drew everything Long Binh would give him; its marksmen held the edges of the town against the 341st Division's infantry crossing the rubber and the open ground east of the town on 9–11 April, and the 43rd Regiment's account of the first three days records the PAVN assault battalions going to ground at 400 metres under fire they could not locate. It held for twelve days, as it held historically²⁵³. The ammunition that might have let it hold longer had gone in March, in the divisional hoards at Hue, Da Nang and Qui Nhon that were abandoned when I Corps and the coast collapsed: about 9 million rounds of linked 5.56mm and most of the match 7.62mm in the country, captured intact¹⁸⁴.

The PAVN changed its approach, bypassed and isolated the town, and on 19 April the Joint General Staff ordered the withdrawal; Xuan Loc was abandoned on 21 April, and the 3rd Battalion of the 1st Airborne Brigade was destroyed in the withdrawal at Suoi Ca. The PAVN claimed 2,036 ARVN killed or wounded and 2,731 captured and admitted 460 killed and 1,428 wounded; Americans estimated about 5,000 PAVN casualties and 37 tanks destroyed²⁵³. I do not adjust these figures; the squad weapons raised the price of each assault and the PAVN answered, as it did historically, by not making more of them¹⁸⁴. On 17 April Phnom Penh fell to the Khmer Rouge. Xuan Loc was the ARVN's last stand, and showed what a well-led division could still do; it bought twelve days.

#### Thieu resigns; the last governments of the Republic
*10–28 Apr 1975 · Saigon and Washington · historical · EV000-97*
On 10 April President Ford asked Congress for $722 million in emergency military aid to rebuild some of the destroyed South Vietnamese forces; Congress would not vote it. On 23 April Ford told an audience at Tulane University that the war was "finished as far as America is concerned". On 21 April Thieu resigned in a televised address denouncing the United States for abandoning his country, and handed power to Vice President Tran Van Huong. Thieu left the country a few days later. On 28 April Huong handed the presidency to General Duong Van Minh, in the hope that Hanoi would negotiate with him; it would not. On 28 April captured A-37s flown by defecting pilots bombed Tan Son Nhut²⁵⁴.

#### The fall of Saigon
*29–30 Apr 1975 · Saigon · historical · EV000-98*
By late April five PAVN corps, Group 232 and the 1st, 2nd, 3rd and 4th Corps, with 15 infantry divisions and three tank regiments, encircled Saigon in the Ho Chi Minh Campaign. Rockets and artillery hit Tan Son Nhut on the morning of 29 April; two US Marine guards, Corporal Charles McMahon and Lance Corporal Darwin Judge, were killed, the last American servicemen killed in action in Vietnam. At 10:51 Operation Frequent Wind began: Marine and Air Force helicopters lifted 395 Americans and more than 4,000 Vietnamese from the Defense Attaché Office compound, and 978 Americans and about 1,100 Vietnamese from the embassy roof and grounds, to the ships of the Seventh Fleet. Ambassador Graham Martin left on a helicopter at 04:58 on 30 April²⁵⁴. Thousands of Vietnamese who had worked with the Americans were left behind.

At 10:24 on 30 April Minh announced an unconditional surrender. PAVN tanks crashed through the gates of the Presidential Palace, Tank 390 through the main gate, and at 11:30 a soldier raised the flag of the Front over the palace²⁵⁴. The Republic of Vietnam had ceased to exist. Saigon was renamed Ho Chi Minh City; Vietnam was formally reunified as the Socialist Republic of Vietnam in July 1976.

#### How the phase ends
The war ends on 30 April 1975 with the fall of Saigon and the unconditional surrender of the Republic of Vietnam, on the historical timeline: rung (a), collapse. The Paris Agreement had removed the American forces and kept the PAVN in the South; Congress had written the end of American combat and the limits on the President into law; American military aid had fallen; and the government in Saigon, more stable than at any time since 1963 but narrowly based, economically weakened and dependent on American supply, faced an enemy that had rebuilt after 1972 and chose its moment. The decisive blows were Hanoi's, at Phuoc Long and Ban Me Thuot; the decisive errors were Saigon's, in the order to abandon the Highlands and the contradictory orders to I Corps.

Hanoi's posture never changed. It was committed to armed reunification throughout, from the Ninth Plenum of December 1963 to the Ho Chi Minh Campaign, through the losses of 1965–68 and 1972, and it accepted the Paris Agreement as a step towards that end. The US war in Vietnam, from the advisory years to 1973, cost more than 58,000 American dead. The Vietnamese dead, military and civilian, northern and southern, are counted in the millions by most estimates, and the record of them is less precise than the record of the Americans. That is the war as it happened, and the war the exercise begins from.

The squad guns and the marksmen's rifles of the ARVN, and about 9 million rounds of linked 5.56mm from the divisional hoards of I and II Corps, are in the PAVN's hands by the end of April 1975, with the carbines of a million men¹⁸⁴.

### Mechanism

Phase VI is where the provision's counter-directional finding comes due. The army that met the ration of January 1974 had been built for ten years, four longer than the historical one, around the belt-fed gun, the thirty-round magazine and the abundance of American ammunition. When the Joint General Staff cut each squad to its basic load for a month, the squad gun went silent first, and battalions that had learned their trade breaking contacts with volume began to avoid contacts they could not break. The historical ARVN had the same habit; this one had it deeper and had a weapon in every squad that ate ammunition faster. The rifles did not cause the shortage. They made the army feel it more.

The bottleneck migrated for the last time, and into the depots. Divisions hoarded linked 5.56mm against the ration and against orders, about nine million rounds in I and II Corps by the end of 1974, and in March 1975 the hoards were lost intact with Hue, Da Nang and Qui Nhon. At Xuan Loc the 18th Division fought on what Long Binh could still send. Its marksmen made the 341st Division's battalions go to ground at four hundred metres east of the town, and it held for twelve days, as it held historically. The PAVN answered as it did historically, by going round.

The provision's positive effect does not vanish in this phase; it becomes irrelevant to the outcome. On the ridges west of Thuong Duc the Airborne's marksmen and squad guns held Hill 1062 against three night assaults with a belt a gun. They held, and the PAVN's unrationed 130mm guns bled them.

### Enemy response and initiative

The PAVN's response to a better-armed ARVN in 1974–75 was the one it had made since 1968: fight it with artillery and go round it. The strategic raids of 1974 were designed to grind down the ARVN's reserves and its stocks, and they worked because the PAVN's artillery was supplied and the ARVN's was not. In 1975 the PAVN captured the ARVN's carbines, squad guns, marksman rifles and the divisional hoards of ammunition in I and II Corps intact, and used some of them in the last weeks of April.

### Causal chains

**C004-5** [war-shaping] From 1964 the ARVN is built around the belt-fed squad gun and abundant American ammunition, four years longer than the historical army → the ration of January 1974 silences the squad gun first and leaves battalions that cannot break contacts by volume avoiding them → division commanders in I and II Corps hoard linked 5.56mm in their own ammunition points against the ration and against Joint General Staff orders, about nine million rounds by the end of 1974 → in March 1975 the hoards are abandoned intact at Hue, Da Nang and Qui Nhon in the collapse of I Corps and the coast, while the 18th Division at Xuan Loc fights on what the Long Binh depot can still send: hoarding against a shortage puts the reserve exactly where the collapse takes it.

### Downstream

There is no later phase. The war ends on 30 April 1975 with the Republic's rifles, squad guns, marksman rifles and about nine million rounds of its ammunition in the PAVN's hands.

### Clock

By 1974 the American clock that mattered was the appropriation, not the casualty list, and the provision bears on it in one way: an army built around the squad gun needs more ammunition for the same fighting, so the $700 million of fiscal 1975 bought a ration that this army felt more sharply than the historical one did²⁴⁷. There were no American combat casualties left to affect. Congress did not cut the aid because of rifles, and would not have restored it for them.

## §4.H Historical comparison

The cumulative force now differs from history in four ways, and this memorandum adds the fourth. The land programme, the police and the provincial chiefs, and the aircraft that see the rear changed the war's interior and the honesty of its accounts; the rifles change the men at the outpost wall and the price of every close fight on our side.

Concretely, against history. The territorials are not outgunned from 1964 to 1969, the five years in which, historically, the countryside was lost, and in the held belt a post with its platoon holds about nine attacks in ten. The general reserve loses no battalion outright in 1965: the 4th Marine Battalion at Binh Gia loses about 65 killed instead of 122, about 280 of the 1st Battalion, 51st come back from Ba Gia, and about 270 of the 7th Airborne Battalion come back from Thuan Loi instead of about 150. At Albany about 30 more Americans live, and in the Hill Fights about 30 more Marines, none of them beside a jammed rifle; there is no M16 scandal and no congressional inquiry¹⁸⁴. At Tet about a third of the attacks on the district towns fail at the walls, and the Front's district-level local forces in the Delta are destroyed in the first night. Over the war I put the lives saved at about 1,000–2,500 American and 10,000–25,000 South Vietnamese¹⁶⁴. That margin is substantial in human terms, a few percent of the war's cost, and almost all of it is in the years 1964–68 before the historical army caught up.

Against these gains: the Front took 10,000–30,000 modern carbines from us by the end of 1965 and fired our ammunition from them for the rest of the war¹⁸⁴; the rearmed Regional Forces were borrowed by the corps in 1966 and twenty-three outposts fell for it; and the army that met the ration of 1974 was more dependent on abundant ammunition than the historical one, and lost its hoards with I Corps. Every main-force battle is fought on the enemy's terms as it was historically. Tet's verdict in Washington, the decision of 31 March, the withdrawal, the Easter Offensive, the Paris Agreement, the aid cuts and the fall of Saigon on 30 April 1975 all stand.

## §4.N Net assessment and end state

The end state is unchanged: rung (a), collapse, on the historical timeline. The Republic falls on 30 April 1975.

The cumulative force now makes the Republic a better state in the hamlets it holds at night than it ever was historically, and its soldiers better armed than their enemies from the first year. That is not enough to reach rung (b), and the reason is the same one the three earlier memoranda gave, seen from a fourth side. Rung (b) needs a Republic that holds while American forces are present and falls only after they leave. This Republic holds where it is present, and it falls on the historical day after the Americans leave, so the difference between (a) and (b) is not about whether it can fight. It is about whether the war ends differently while the Americans are there: whether Hanoi's will, its sanctuaries or the American clock move. None of the four provisions touches any of them. The rifles make the price of every close fight lower on our side and give the enemy no reason to fight differently, except closer and at night and with artillery. Rung (c), assisted viability, would need an RVNAF that defeats a conventional offensive with American air power and supply. In 1972 it did that historically, and in this war it does the same, no better. The rifles have no bearing on whether the supply continues after 1973.

The distance between (c) and (d) is still the whole war, and the rifles widen it slightly in one direction. An army that has never fought without abundant American ammunition is further from self-sufficiency, not closer, and the counter-directional finding of Part 2 holds in the record.

No phase redraw is proposed. The shape of the war is unchanged; its interior is changed in Phases I–III.

## §5 Delta ledger

- **Forces gained:** P004a, M4A1 URG-I carbines with Aimpoint CompM5 sights for all US forces, the RVNAF and the RF/PF, about 310,000 issued by June 1964 and about 1.7–1.9 million cumulative by 1972; P004b, M110A2 marksman rifles, one per US and ARVN infantry squad, about 13,000 cumulative; P004c, M249 squad guns, about 17,000–19,000 cumulative. US spaces about 470¹⁶⁴.
- **Forces lost:** between 10,000 and 30,000 M4A1s to the Front by the end of 1965; about 300 more from the Chuong Thien posts in 1966; the whole inventory, and about 9 million rounds of linked 5.56mm from the divisional hoards, to the PAVN in March–April 1975¹⁸⁴.
- **End vectors moved:** Phase I, ARVN effectiveness 0 → +1 and night control of the hamlets 0 → +1; Phase II, ARVN effectiveness 0 → +1; Phase III, ARVN effectiveness 0 → +1. Phases IV–VI unchanged on every headline variable.
- **Off-vector variables pushed in Phases I–III:** RF/PF effectiveness +1; US unit quality +1; FIX capability +1; VC local force +1 (rearmed from our stocks); LOG distribution −1. US casualties and US political tolerance move inside their bands and are not marked.
- **New events:** EV004-1 Operation IRON GATE (1964); EV004-2 the Regional Force companies go to the corps (1966); EV004-3 the lot numbers (1966). Historical events altered: Binh Gia, Ba Gia, Dong Xoai, the Ia Drang, the Hill Fights, Hanoi's decision on the general offensive, the first wave of Tet, Hue, the mobilization of 1968, the shortages of 1974, the attrition of 1974 and Xuan Loc; the police posts' event of 1964–65 extended.
- **New category:** none. Infantry small arms existed historically; this changes their timing and kind.
- **End state:** unchanged, rung (a).

## §6 Changed / not changed

### What has changed

- The men who hold the hamlets at night are not outgunned from 1964, five years before the historical rearmament of the territorials. In the held belt, posts with their platoon hold about nine attacks in ten, and the land and police programmes are protected by garrisons that win the first thirty seconds. This is the largest effect of the memorandum, and no statistic this headquarters kept shows it.
- The general reserve survives 1965 whole. Binh Gia, Ba Gia and Dong Xoai are lost at about half their historical cost, and no battalion is wiped out.
- At Tet about a third of the attacks on the district towns fail at the compound walls, and the Front's district-level local forces in the Delta are destroyed in the first night.
- The 1967 M16 crisis never happens: no Marines die beside jammed rifles on 881 South, no subcommittee sits, and there are no letters home about a rifle that jams. Over the war about 1,000–2,500 American and 10,000–25,000 South Vietnamese lives are saved, almost all of them before 1969.
- The Commission can trace ammunition from a battlefield to a depot by its lot number, and in 1966 it convicts the men who sold it. CORDS's 1967 rule that a Regional Force company belongs to its province is written from this war's own losses.

### What has not changed

- The enemy still chooses when and where to fight. Every main-force battle in the record is still fought on his terms, and the PAVN's answer to better rifles is to fight closer, at night, and with artillery.
- Neither half of the problem of finding the enemy is touched. The main force under the canopy and the infrastructure in the hamlet are as hidden as before.
- The Front rearms from our stocks: 10,000–30,000 carbines by the end of 1965, and our ammunition bought in the towns for the rest of the war.
- Tet's verdict in Washington, the decision of 31 March, the withdrawal, the Easter Offensive, the Paris Agreement and the aid cuts all fall on their historical dates.
- The army is more dependent on American ammunition, not less. The ration of 1974 falls on it harder, and its hoards are lost with I Corps.
- Saigon falls on 30 April 1975. Rung (a).

### Trajectory Log entry

Memorandum 004 gave every American and South Vietnamese soldier and every territorial a modern carbine from 1964, with a belt-fed gun and a marksman in each regular squad. Its effect was concentrated where the war was decided first: at the outpost wall after dark. In the held belt a post with its platoon now held nine attacks in ten, and the land and police programmes had garrisons that won the first thirty seconds. The general reserve came through 1965 battered but whole, the M16 crisis of 1967 never happened, and at Tet a third of the attacks on the district towns failed at the walls. Perhaps 10,000–25,000 South Vietnamese and 1,000–2,500 American lives were saved, mostly before 1969, when the historical army caught up. Against this, the Front armed itself with our carbines, and the army that met the 1974 ration was more dependent on American ammunition and lost its hoards with I Corps. The enemy still chose every battle. Saigon falls on 30 April 1975, at rung (a).

### Positions recorded

- **POS004-1** · vars: RFPF.effectiveness, POP.night_control, ENEMY.engagement_control · Infantry small arms change how the fights the enemy accepts go, never which fights he accepts; at the outpost their value equals the platoon's presence after dark and the reaction behind it, and the enemy's answer to a better-armed post is to attack what is not rearmed (004 F004-1, F004-3, F004-16; §4.P1)
- **POS004-2** · vars: ARVN.effectiveness, US.unit_quality, US.political_tolerance · In this war the ARVN and the RF/PF carry modern carbines from 1964, and the 1967 M16 crisis and inquiry and the 1968–69 rearmament do not occur; from 1969 the historical army has caught up, and the remaining difference is the squad gun, the marksman and the practice (004 F004-6; §4.P2–P4)
- **POS004-3** · vars: ARVN.self_sufficiency, LOG.distribution · Abundant American ammunition from 1964 deepens the RVNAF's dependence on volume of fire; the 1974 ration falls hardest on the squad gun, and the divisional hoards built against it are lost in the collapse of March 1975 (004 F004-15; §4.P6)

### Persons recorded

- **Arthur L. Venn** · Lieutenant Colonel, US Army; chief of the MACV small-arms new-equipment training team from January 1964 · invented in 004 · Ran Operation IRON GATE under the rule that no new weapon left a depot until the old one came in and no carbine went to a post unzeroed; refused to shorten the course for the corps commanders
- **Lam Van Thien** · Lieutenant Colonel, province chief of Chuong Thien in 1966 · invented in 004 · Protested in writing the corps' borrowing of his Regional Force companies in April 1966; his returns of the posts lost became the case for the 1967 rule that RF companies belong to their province
- **Truong Vinh Khoa** · Commissioner Against Corruption from 1 January 1964, formerly a judge of the Saigon Court of Appeal · invented in 001 · Refused to charge the Dalat generals without evidence, convicted the Sa Dec ammunition ring in 1967 on cartridge lot numbers and a general the same year, audited Phoenix from 1968, kept in office but placed under the presidential Inspectorate in 1971
